Title: Web Agent Agentic Reinforcement Learning Decision Model Under Multi-Cost and Failure Risk

  • Authors: Qianli Ma, Limengxi Yue, Shuyang Xu, Yanpei Shi, Hongrui Liu
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23857
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.23857
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.23857]] Web Agent Agentic Reinforcement Learning Decision Model Under Multi-Cost and Failure Risk(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23857)
  • Keywords: extraction
  • Abstract: Intelligent agent interactions in real-world web environments are commonly constrained by request budgets, time delays, anti-crawling restrictions, and operational failure risks. Strategies solely optimizing task success rates often exhibit unusable phenomena such as "high success but high cost" or "low risk but conservative failure." This paper proposes a constrained agentic reinforcement learning model for web agents, unifying page access, search requests, and external API calls into a unified long-term decision-making framework with associated costs. It simultaneously incorporates cost budget constraints and tail risk control into the optimization objective: constructing a multidimensional cost vector comprising cumulative request count, total latency, and failure penalties to achieve budget compliance via Lagrange dual updates, while employing a CVaR risk term to suppress excessive exploration of high-failure-probability paths, thereby achieving an adaptive balance among "completion rate, cost, and risk." Experiments were conducted across 30-70 site/page templates and 800-1,500 end-to-end web tasks, including information extraction, price comparison, form submission, and cross-page navigation. Interaction sequences spanned 20-120 steps with tool scales of 30-200. Performance was benchmarked against unconstrained RL, budget-constrained RL, and rule-based/scripted web agents, quantifying task completion rates, cost-per-success, failure rates, and policy stability. Results demonstrate that at equivalent completion rates, our method reduces C-PS by 22%-31% and lowers failure rates by 18%-26% under high failure penalties. Under fixed budgets, task completion rates increase by 10%-16%, highlighting the necessity and effectiveness of constraint modeling for practical web agent deployment.

Title: I$^2$-Med: Interpretable Medical Inference through Visual-Guided Dynamic Logits Calibration

  • Authors: Md Sajid, Shreeyut Maheshwari, Akshat Mishra, Ritik Mishra, Mohammad Tanveer
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23858
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.23858
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.23858]] I$^2$-Med: Interpretable Medical Inference through Visual-Guided Dynamic Logits Calibration(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23858)
  • Keywords: interpretability
  • Abstract: Medical vision language models have demonstrated strong potential for medical image understanding and reasoning. However, their interpretability and reliability remain limited because medical vision language tasks require precise visual grounding and clinically coherent explanations. This is particularly challenging due to complex imaging patterns and scarce expert annotations. These challenges reduce the effectiveness of conventional reasoning methods and lead to visual hallucination, defined as the generation of text that is not grounded in the given visual input, which further limits their applicability in clinical practice. To mitigate these challenges, we propose an interpretable medical inference (I$^2$-Med) framework that enhances visual faithfulness during inference without additional training to improve reasoning and explanation faithfulness in medical vision language models. During text generation, I$^2$-Med uses a visual guidance module to check whether each candidate word is supported by the medical image and consistent with the current reasoning. Based on this guidance, the model's output scores are adjusted to favor visually grounded words, without changing the model parameters. Extensive experiments on the OmniMedVQA benchmark, covering eight medical imaging modalities and five clinical question types, demonstrate that our proposed I$^2$-Med framework generates more clinically meaningful and visually faithful reasoning chains and reduces visual hallucination under standard medical VQA evaluation metrics. The source code is publicly available at \url{this https URL}.

Title: Modeling Human Behavior in the Prisoner's Dilemma with Large Language Models

  • Authors: Raina Gao, Tristan Raffo
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.GT
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23860
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.23860
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.23860]] Modeling Human Behavior in the Prisoner's Dilemma with Large Language Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23860)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: The Prisoner's Dilemma has long served as a foundational framework for studying human decision-making and cooperative outcomes across social scenarios, yet human behavior consistently deviates from predictions of fully rational economic theory. Previous works have studied human and LLM outcomes in the Prisoner's Dilemma; however, these studies have lacked at least one key feature: a memory component for LLM agents, accessible open-source models, or comparison against empirical human data. This project investigates whether open-source LLMs, TII Falcon 7B Instruct, Google Gemma 7B, Meta Llama 3.1 8B Instruct, Microsoft Phi Mini MoE Instruct, and Qwen 2.5 7B Instruct, can model human cooperation patterns in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games, while examining how an LLM's knowledge that its opponent will be shuffled affects its decisions. Using a standardized prompting framework, we simulated interactions in two trial types: a fixed-partner setting where both agents retain memory, and a shuffled-partner setting where a memory-enabled agent, informed that its opponent changes across rounds, interacts with a memoryless agent. For each trial, we measured cooperation rates over 100 rounds and 10-round increments and compared them to human data from Montero-Porras et al. (2022). Under both frameworks, all LLMs differed from the human cooperation rate. Open-source LLMs generally over-cooperated relative to humans, particularly in the shuffled framework, where human cooperation averaged 28.5% while several models exceeded 80%. Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct showed deterministic tendencies, while Phi-mini-MoE-Instruct displayed greater variability and briefly approximated human rates. These findings suggest that LLMs have potential as behavioral simulation agents and provide a benchmark across two interaction environments, although open-source models are not yet reliable substitutes for human participants.

Title: Composable Parametric Demographic Injection for Values Survey Simulation

  • Authors: Xinyuan Cao, Qingyi Pan, Bangde Du, Yujia Zhou, Hexi Wang, Weihang Su, Yiqun Liu, Yueyue Wu, Min Zhang, Qingyao Ai
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23867
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.23867
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.23867]] Composable Parametric Demographic Injection for Values Survey Simulation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23867)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used as simulated respondents in computational social science, yet accurately reproducing population-specific heterogeneous response distributions remains challenging. Existing methods such as demographic prompting are sensitive to prompt design and increase inference overhead, while parameter-based methods struggle to reuse demographic information efficiently, limiting generalization to unseen combinations. In this regard, we propose PARADE, a composable PARAmetric DEmographic injection framework that represents each demographic attribute value with a reusable and composable LoRA module. Specifically, PARADE separates task-common survey capability from demographic-specific value shifts via a shared Task LoRA and attribute-level Demographic LoRAs, with composition calibration to reduce parameter interference. At inference time, target population models can be constructed on demand by recombining the relevant modules, including demographic combinations not observed during training. Extensive experiments on the World Values Survey and SocioBench across three backbone models show that PARADE achieves strong population-level distribution alignment while maintaining competitive predictive performance, reducing KL divergence by up to 52.8% relative to the strongest baseline.

Title: EMMA: Event-Driven Multimodal Memory System with Multi-Agent Retrieval

  • Authors: Ruochen Zhao, Jiazheng Kang, Gezi Li, Dongying Lin, Ting Bai
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23892
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.23892
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.23892]] EMMA: Event-Driven Multimodal Memory System with Multi-Agent Retrieval(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23892)
  • Keywords: large language model, segmentation
  • Abstract: Long video understanding requires reasoning over hours of content that far exceed the context window of multimodal large language models (MLLMs). Existing approaches compress video into fixed-length clips, yet events are fragmented and unrelated content is merged, yielding noisy retrieval. To overcome this challenge, we propose EMMA, an Event-driven Multimodal Memory system with Multi-Agent retrieval, which organizes memory around semantic events in three stages: (1) Event segmentation: a dual-stream detector adaptively locates event boundaries by fusing visual semantic drift with spoken transitions, rather than fixed windows. (2) Memory construction: built on the detected events, a three-level hierarchy of raw video, clip-level memory, and event-level memory stores content at progressively higher semantic abstraction. (3) Memory retrieval: a multi-agent framework collaboratively queries this hierarchy through query decomposition, tiered search, evidence validation, and answer generation, narrowing evidence from coarse events to precise clips. Extensive experiments on three challenging benchmarks demonstrate substantial improvements, showing the effectiveness of our event-aligned memory system. Code, prompt templates, and reproduction scripts are available at this https URL.

Title: Path-Conditioned Attention Mobility: From Depth to Execution Hubs in SegFormer

  • Authors: Aleksandr Druzhinin
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23893
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.23893
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.23893]] Path-Conditioned Attention Mobility: From Depth to Execution Hubs in SegFormer(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23893)
  • Keywords: transformer, segmentation
  • Abstract: Transformer compression often couples two distinct quantities: retained attention capacity and the number of sequential depths at which that capacity is executed. We study whether useful attention functions in a hierarchical vision transformer are locally mobile across depth. Using SegFormer-B5 for human segmentation, we find that some task-relevant attention heads can be relocated to nearby depths, but only when their computational context is respected. Head-only relocation is insufficient, while joint adaptation of the relocated head and surrounding representation boundary improves functional preservation; replacing donor-specific normalization and spatial-reduction preprocessing causes large degradation. We use this path-conditioned mobility to reorganize retained attention from depth into width. Starting from a fixed parent with 120 retained Stage-3 heads across 40 attention execution depths, we consolidate the same 120-head budget into 20 execution hubs. Under a layout-matched three-seed recovery control, K20 reaches 0.960686 +/- 0.000059 mIoU versus 0.960400 +/- 0.000074 for the corresponding State120 control. The paired point difference is +0.000285, while a paired image-bootstrap confidence interval includes zero; we therefore interpret the result as no measurable quality penalty rather than statistical superiority. For deployment, FOLDED_VEC preserves donor-specific paths while vectorizing their execution. Relative to the same 120-head State120 parent, it reduces end-to-end NVIDIA L4 latency by 5.28%, 3.15%, and 3.58% at batch sizes 1, 4, and 8. These results suggest that attention redundancy includes not only removable capacity but also execution-depth redundancy, provided that the computational path defining a relocated function is preserved.

Title: RhinoNet: Unsupervised Learning for Phenoscaping and Generation of High Dimensional Point Clouds

  • Authors: Siddharth Viswanath, Hiren Madhu, Eddie Cavallin, Benjamin Jones, Alistair Wilkinson, Christopher Tape, Michael Perlmutter, Rex Ying, Ian Adelstein, Smita Krishnaswamy
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23898
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.23898
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.23898]] RhinoNet: Unsupervised Learning for Phenoscaping and Generation of High Dimensional Point Clouds(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23898)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: We present RhinoNet, a simplicial complex-based point cloud neural network, that creates a geometry-preserving embedding of high-dimensional point clouds. RhinoNet takes collections of datasets, also called {\em data cohorts}, and processes each dataset (point cloud) within the cohort as a simplicial complex. These simplicial complexes have datapoints as vertices and edges learned by Vietoris-Rips filtration. Using simplicial wavelets and MLP layers, RhinoNet computes a vector representation of each dataset in a latent space produced by an autoencoder. The latent space is optimized such that an optimal transport distance between pairs of point clouds is preserved between the latent representations. We apply RhinoNet to single-cell datasets collected on patients---a common source of such high-dimensional data. RhinoNet creates a patient level representation, known in biological contexts as a phenoscape. We establish that RhinoNet generates organized phenoscapes, whereas other methods such as static maps do not. Next we show that we can generate entire point clouds by sampling and decoding this phenoscape. We show two modes of generation via 1) latent flow matching and 2) geodesic interpolation in latent space, followed by a decoding back into the point cloud space. RhinoNet achieves state-of-the-art performance on single-cell and spatial data cohorts relative to tested baselines. Overall, RhinoNet lifts the concepts of representation learning and generative modeling to the point cloud or population level and defines a neural model for phenoscaping.

Title: EAGLE: Entropy-Aware Geometric Learning Enhancer for Partially Polygon-Supervised Object Localization

  • Authors: Naveen Stewart
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23900
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.23900
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.23900]] EAGLE: Entropy-Aware Geometric Learning Enhancer for Partially Polygon-Supervised Object Localization(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23900)
  • Keywords: segmentation
  • Abstract: Object detection is a critical application of computer vision, yet models trained uniformly on bounding boxes lack the geometric precision to be fully effective in inference settings, especially at high IoU thresholds. One common approach is the introduction of polygon-supervised localization, where a model is trained explicitly for localization but with access to polygon masks during training. Among such methods, partial polygon supervision is viable due to the higher annotation costs of developing fully supervised training datasets. However, this introduces a further challenge: in object classes of relatively low shape complexity, the addition of training masks and subsequent segmentation loss may obscure box localization, often worsening model performance despite access to deeper geometric information. To address the practical limitations of both full and partial polygon supervision, we introduce a mathematical framework for quantifying per-class geometric complexity by combining information theory and statistical shape analysis. With this framework, we develop Entropy-Aware Geometric Learning Enhancer (EAGLE), a lightweight, post-training model head that learns geometric information for the localization model, improving accuracy via edge refinement and localization rescoring using entropy-informed updates. Empirical analysis across MS COCO and Pascal SBD demonstrates the entropy framework meaningfully predicts shape reconstruction error and accuracy differences among mask-derived and standard box predictions, while the implemented EAGLE framework achieves consistent improvement on already SOTA detection frameworks (+0.9 mAP50-95 points on MS COCO) at minimal observed inference overhead.

Title: Rethinking weakly supervised localization on chest radiographs: position nulls, protocol dependence, and PACE

  • Authors: Shixin Qiu
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23902
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.23902
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.23902]] Rethinking weakly supervised localization on chest radiographs: position nulls, protocol dependence, and PACE(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23902)
  • Keywords: extraction
  • Abstract: Weakly supervised localization on chest radiographs is reported as a single overlap accuracy, and those numbers are not comparable across papers. We measure what one contains. Against position-null controls that keep a readout's own box and move only its position, class activation mapping and its gradient-weighted variant beat the null on two to four of seven annotated findings on ChestX-ray14 and two of eight on CheXlocalize. The null is beatable: three board-certified radiologists clear it on four to seven. Three rarely stated choices each move a result by more than most reported method effects: the results regime, the extraction threshold, and the mask-to-box rule, whose cost is predicted by how fragmented the thresholded mask is. Predicted box area alone reverses a paired comparison. These measurements produced PACE, for \emph{population anomaly and class evidence}, which multiplies the finding identity from the label-trained head by the spatial evidence from a frozen radiograph backbone that never sees a pathology label. It is the case study that made the protocol dependence visible, and we report its costs beside its gain: in an exploratory matched-area analysis it improves strict-overlap accuracy on both corpora and beats a label-derived second map, but it is the least faithful of the three readouts to the classifier's causal map, its advantage depends on the box rule, and, this benchmark releasing no localization validation set, every design choice behind it was made on the boxes it is scored on. Its one clean transfer beats one baseline and loses to the other.

Title: LVMH: A Meta-Optimization Harness for Long-Video Agent Memory

  • Authors: Liwei Che, Benlin Liu, Dai Li, Xiangyan Li, Zijian Wu, Changjiang Li, Minghao Guo, Chengzhi Mao, Vladimir Pavlovic, Ranjay Krishna
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.MA
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23904
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.23904
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.23904]] LVMH: A Meta-Optimization Harness for Long-Video Agent Memory(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23904)
  • Keywords: protect
  • Abstract: Long-video agents must map hours or days of multimodal evidence into a bounded context while respecting token and latency budgets, making memory design a context-management challenge. Existing memory systems largely rely on hand-designed policies, leaving open how to jointly optimize memory design for performance and efficiency. We propose Long Video Meta-memory Harness (LVMH), an automated multi-agent framework that formulates memory-policy development as multi-objective code search around a fixed answer model. To guard against self-certification and development-set overfitting, LVMH separates implementation from independent review and enforces protected observation boundaries. Persistent meta-memory records past proposals, traces, costs, and failures to guide subsequent policy revisions. Through this harness, we discover Self-Evolving Evidence Memory (SEEM), a "text-first, vision-second" policy that answers from compact structured event memory and evidence specialists, consulting localized frames through a selective visual gate. The policy evolved on EgoLifeQA generalizes to unseen benchmarks, outperforming Meta-Harness on both Ego-R1 Bench and Video-MME (L) with GPT-5 mini. Across the three benchmarks, SEEM with GPT-5 achieves 65.7% average accuracy, surpassing the previous state of the art by 7.7 percentage points with approximately 32x lower average evaluation token use and 91x shorter average response time.

Title: Instance-Specific Classifier-Free Guidance for Text-to-Image Diffusion/Flow-Based Editing

  • Authors: Wajeeh Atrash, Tom Tirer
  • Subjects: cs.CV, eess.IV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23905
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.23905
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.23905]] Instance-Specific Classifier-Free Guidance for Text-to-Image Diffusion/Flow-Based Editing(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23905)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: In recent years, a variety of prompt-based image editing methods have been developed based on text-to-image (T2I) diffusion/flow models. Image editing introduces an inherent trade-off between adhering to the original source image and realizing the change specified by the target prompt, which are typically measured by the LPIPS and CLIP-T metrics, respectively. Intuitively, this trade-off can be controlled by the classifier-free guidance (CFG) scale. Most existing editing methods, though, use a fixed CFG scale, ignoring that different edits require varying levels of modification. In this paper, we first explore the potential benefits of using an instance-specific CFG by exhaustively re-running prominent editing methods with a wide range of CFG scale values on benchmark datasets. We show that instance-specific setting can significantly improve LPIPS without compromising CLIP-T. Yet, tuning based on repeatedly executing an editing pipeline is inefficient. To prevent slow inference, we train a light-weight neural network that predicts the metrics of the underlying editing method for a given edit instance and CFG scale. At inference time, these predictions enable fast selection of an instance-specific CFG scale. Experiments across multiple editing methods and benchmark settings show the performance advantage of our approach over the baseline methods.

Title: Beyond Generator-Specific Shortcuts: Open-World AI-Generated Video Detection via Adversarial Training

  • Authors: Qi Zhou, Yuanfan Li, Huiqi Deng, Hongbin Pei, Yazhe Tang
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23906
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.23906
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.23906]] Beyond Generator-Specific Shortcuts: Open-World AI-Generated Video Detection via Adversarial Training(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23906)
  • Keywords: attack, robust
  • Abstract: AI-generated video (AIGV) detection is increasingly important for mitigating misinformation, impersonation, and other malicious uses of synthetic media, yet existing detectors often generalize poorly to unseen generators in open-world settings. To better understand this challenge, we analyze the failure modes of AIGV detectors under cross-generator out-of-distribution (OOD) shifts and observe that detector representations strongly encode generator-specific cues, which are associated with substantial ID-OOD performance drops. Further analysis shows that naively perturbing generator-sensitive tokens can even reduce OOD performance, indicating that generator-related cues and detection-relevant evidence are only partially separable. Motivated by these findings, we propose Forensic Obfuscation of geneRator-specific cues via Gradient-guided Embedding perturbation (FORGE), a token-aware adversarial training framework for open-world AIGV detection. FORGE identifies tokens that are strongly associated with generator-specific shortcuts yet less critical to real/fake discrimination, and selectively perturbs them during training to suppress generator-dependent patterns while preserving useful forensic evidence. Through synchronized adversarial training between an attacker and a detector, FORGE encourages the detector to learn more transferable generator-invariant forensic representations. Experiments on 29 held-out benchmark entries show that FORGE achieves an average F1 of 92.62, outperforming eight strong baselines by 5.31 points, while also remaining robust under four common post-processing attacks. We will release our code and datasets upon acceptance.

Title: When the Editing Intent Is Split: A Cross-Modal Jailbreak Attack for Large Image Editing Models

  • Authors: Xianmin Ye, Zhiyuan Fan, Tianju Liu, Yuhang Wang, Zhenxing Niu, Haichang Gao
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23909
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.23909
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.23909]] When the Editing Intent Is Split: A Cross-Modal Jailbreak Attack for Large Image Editing Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23909)
  • Keywords: defense, attack
  • Abstract: Large image editing models increasingly infer editing intent from both textual and visual inputs, creating an underexplored safety risk when harmful intent is distributed across modalities. In this work, we formalize this split-intent vulnerability and propose the Split-Intent Jailbreak Attack (SIJA), which asymmetrically distributes an unsafe editing intent by specifying the editing referent in text while concealing the safety-critical action and specification through graphical visual semantics in an auxiliary cue. To systematically study this threat, we introduce the Split-Intent Image Editing Safety Benchmark (SI2ESBench) for evaluating image editing models under split-intent inputs. Extensive experiments on representative commercial and open-source models demonstrate that SIJA effectively compromises state-of-the-art image editing systems, achieving ASRs of 68.28% on GPT Image 2 and 91.38% on Qwen-Image-3.0-Pro. Our results expose a mismatch between multimodal editing and safety assessment: the distributed intent can be recovered to execute the requested transformation, yet its harmfulness often remains undetected by existing safeguards. To mitigate this vulnerability, we propose editing-intent reconstruction, a defense principle that explicitly evaluates the complete transformation implied by the joint multimodal context and substantially improves rejection of split-intent attacks. Our findings expose a cross-modal safety gap in modern image editing systems and highlight the need to assess the jointly inferred transformation rather than individual inputs in isolation. this https URL

Title: Pseudo-Label Foreground Quality Weighting for Domain-Generalized Fundus Image Segmentation

  • Authors: Lucas Gabriel Telesco, José Ignacio Orlando
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23911
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.23911
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.23911]] Pseudo-Label Foreground Quality Weighting for Domain-Generalized Fundus Image Segmentation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23911)
  • Keywords: segmentation
  • Abstract: Optic disc (OD) and optic cup (OC) segmentation is central to automated glaucoma assessment, but models trained on limited labeled fundus datasets often degrade under domain shift. Semi-supervised Teacher-Student learning can improve generalization by leveraging unlabeled data, yet standard Noisy Student training treats all pseudo-labels as equally reliable. In this paper we investigate a targeted extension of Noisy Student training in which OD- and OC-specific pseudo-label supervision is modulated using a mask-derived reliability estimate. A multitask Dice predictor estimates OD- and OC-specific pseudo-label quality from Teacher-generated masks, and these estimates are used to weight the pseudo-label loss during Student training. This preserves the baseline contribution of low-scoring foreground pseudo-labels while assigning greater relative emphasis to pseudo-labels predicted to be more reliable. Experiments across known and unseen domains show improvements over the Noisy Student baseline, particularly for OC segmentation, while maintaining competitive generalization performance.

Title: Your Self-Supervised Projection Head Captures Object Co-Occurrence Statistics

  • Authors: Arthur Aubret, Jochen Triesch, Céline Teulière
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23915
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.23915
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.23915]] Your Self-Supervised Projection Head Captures Object Co-Occurrence Statistics(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23915)
  • Keywords: segmentation
  • Abstract: Self-Supervised Learning (SSL) has achieved impressive success in learning semantic visual representations, yet the underlying principles driving this success remain underexplored. In this work, we hypothesize that common SSL pretext tasks implicitly model object co-occurrence statistics, a fundamental cue for visual learning. To test this, we curate three datasets of segmented objects from existing vision benchmarks using a state-of-the-art segmentation model. Through experiments across many SSL models, we reveal a hierarchical encoding of semantic information: while the visual backbone captures object categories, the projection head specializes in encoding co-occurrences between object categories. As a result, the projection head outperforms the backbone in aligning with human judgments of inter-category similarity. Furthermore, by controlling co-occurrence patterns during pre-training, we demonstrate that encoding object co-occurrences can significantly accelerate the emergence of category-level representations. Our findings uncover a previously hidden learning principle in SSL and suggest a path toward designing more effective pretext tasks by explicitly leveraging object co-occurrence structure.

Title: miniTE: Diagnosing and Bridging the Execution Gap in Native NVFP4 Reinforcement Learning

  • Authors: Zhen Li, Mingfa Feng, Yiming Zhang, Yang Yu, Changhai Zhou, Pengxiang Li, Dongkuan Wu, Congkai Xie, Hongxia Yang
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, stat.ML
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23928
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.23928
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.23928]] miniTE: Diagnosing and Bridging the Execution Gap in Native NVFP4 Reinforcement Learning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23928)
  • Keywords: transformer, large language model
  • Abstract: Native NVFP4 promises to accelerate reinforcement learning (RL) for large language models (LLMs), but achieving stable learning across separate training and sampling engines remains challenging. We show that matching model weights and numerical formats is insufficient to ensure consistent policy evaluation, highlighting the importance of cross-engine numerical execution. We introduce miniTE, a lightweight framework that combines execution diagnostics, cross-engine numerical alignment, and efficient native W4A4 (4-bit weights and activations) GEMM computation for LLM RL. It improves numerical execution consistency without modifying the RL objective, while reducing implementation overhead to realize the benefits of native low-bit computation. Long-horizon RL experiments on Qwen3-30B-A3B demonstrate stable training and benchmark performance close to BF16, outperforming naive Transformer Engine (TE) NVFP4 in evaluation quality. On single NVIDIA B300 GPU node, the optimized learner reaches 33,540 tokens/s, yielding 1.39x and 1.47x the throughput of BF16 and TE NVFP4, respectively, for complete learner updates. We released the framework, kernels, diagnostics, and training configurations to make native low-bit RL easier.

Title: VERDICT: Visual Extraction-Result Decoder with Integrated Confidence and Traceable Evidence

  • Authors: Harikrishnan P M, Goutham Vignesh, Siva Prasad, Zishan Ahmad, Mahesh Basveshwar Deshmukhe, Varun V, Rohit Agrawal, Vishal Vaddina
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23933
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.23933
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.23933]] VERDICT: Visual Extraction-Result Decoder with Integrated Confidence and Traceable Evidence(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23933)
  • Keywords: extraction
  • Abstract: Enterprises run schema-guided structured field extraction on documents with frontier multimodal models, and accuracy keeps improving as new models and systems arrive. Two things have not kept pace: the evidence for each extracted value, meaning the location of the document it was read from, and how far to trust the value and that evidence. Neither is a component of most extraction systems today, so we build them as a framework that attaches to an existing extractor and leaves it untouched. We present VERDICT, a plug-in framework that uses a small Vision--Language Model (VLM) to ground/localize the evidence once per document over a fixed external extractor's output, then calibrates value and evidence confidence. Fine-tuning the VLM for evidence localization substantially improves visual evidence quality and, more importantly, expands VERDICT's selective-prediction operating range. At a strict confidence threshold $0.80$, VERDICT with the fine-tuned VLM gives usable precision--coverage trade-offs for both value and evidence confidence across the in-distribution and out of distribution datasets, whereas the frozen setting often collapses to little or no coverage. Running an open-weight VLM for localization rather than a closed API makes its internal states available to the calibrator, and they are worth more than output log-probabilities: in-distribution AUROC rises from $0.730$ to $0.914$ for value confidence and from $0.681$ to $0.906$ for evidence confidence. This advantage does not survive a distribution shift. We show that the signal is displaced rather than destroyed, and that labeling roughly $120$ fields from the target dataset takes evidence confidence from $0.641$ to $0.905$ on one dataset and from $0.675$ to $0.829$ on the other.

Title: Organizing LLM Inference for Aggregate Predictions from Behavioral Data

  • Authors: Varun Gupta, Vijay Kamble
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, econ.EM
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23934
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.23934
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.23934]] Organizing LLM Inference for Aggregate Predictions from Behavioral Data(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23934)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: When using a large language model (LLM) to simulate a user population, how should we combine individual behavioral histories to predict the fraction of users who take a particular action? We study this question in e-commerce, predicting observed product-level conversion rates from browsing sessions without task-specific model training. We vary two design choices, each involving a bias-variance tradeoff. Summarizing a product's sessions before inference reduces cost but can discard predictive detail, adding bias, whereas predicting outcomes for individual sessions and averaging afterward preserves that detail but requires many calls to reduce variance. Evaluating products together in one prompt can help the model infer what is typical in the setting, reducing bias, but also makes each prediction depend on which other products appear alongside it, adding noise. We evaluate both choices across five LLMs and two datasets. Our main finding is that predictions from individual sessions outperform predictions from summaries when products are evaluated separately, but summaries lead when products are evaluated together. Comparative summaries achieve the highest Spearman correlation with observed rates in 12 of 15 settings, with no alternative significantly better. Comparison thus helps summaries far more than session predictions, overcoming the advantage of behavioral detail. We interpret this difference through a Bayesian model of the second tradeoff, which suggests that comparison helps more when the inputs in a prompt reveal more about differences across products, consistent with our results for four types of comparative context. Comparative summaries also incur a small fraction of the inference cost of the session methods. With the best model for each outcome, they attain 66-89% of the correlation of the best supervised benchmark trained on thousands of labeled products.

Title: Factorization-Guided Training and Inference for Diffusion-Drafted Speculative Decoding

  • Authors: Jiakun Fan, Min Xiao, Qiang Wang, Neiwen Ling, Zhichao Liu, Zhihao Jia, Dimitrios S. Nikolopoulos, Zijun Wei
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23935
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.23935
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.23935]] Factorization-Guided Training and Inference for Diffusion-Drafted Speculative Decoding(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23935)
  • Keywords: diffusion, large language model
  • Abstract: Speculative decoding accelerates large language model inference through parallel verification of tokens proposed by a drafter. Diffusion drafters further parallelize proposal generation, but greater proposal capacity or higher mean accepted tokens (MAT) need not yield higher throughput. Because verification accepts only a consecutive prefix, early rejections can render later correct predictions ineffective, limiting MAT gains, while even higher MAT may not improve throughput if per-round latency increases. We introduce DScout, which uses two complementary factorizations to guide diffusion drafter training and inference for higher throughput. For training, DScout factorizes position-wise loss into inclusion, weighting, and prediction loss. This guides the design and combination of mixed-horizon training, intra-block unmasking, and acceptance-weighted loss to align supervision with prefix acceptance and improve MAT. For inference, DScout factorizes throughput as the ratio of MAT to per-round latency, guiding target-conditioning depth, drafter depth, and inference block size selection to identify a high-throughput configuration for each workload. Across six benchmarks, DScout achieves $3.84\times$ and $4.14\times$ mean speedups over autoregressive decoding on Qwen3-4B and Qwen3-8B, respectively, improving mean speedup over the state-of-the-art baseline by 9.1% and 7.1%. Across models and concurrency settings, our inference configuration policy provides up to 9.75% additional mean speedup over DScout's fixed configuration.

Title: SECURE-AI: Multi-Signal Uncertainty Quantification for Trustworthy Acoustic Cough Detection

  • Authors: Sejal Solkhan, Feiya Xiang, Mayur Sanap, Geet Khatri, Edgar Lobaton
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.SD, eess.AS
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23937
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.23937
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.23937]] SECURE-AI: Multi-Signal Uncertainty Quantification for Trustworthy Acoustic Cough Detection(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23937)
  • Keywords: secure
  • Abstract: Acoustic models for cough detection achieve high accuracy on curated benchmarks but fail silently in deployment, where speech, environmental noise, and audio unlike the training data induce confident misclassifications. In health monitoring, a confidently wrong prediction is worse than no answer at all. We present SECURE-AI (Self-aware Estimation of Cough Uncertainty for Reliable Evaluation of AI), a multi-signal uncertainty quantification framework that wraps a frozen MobileNetV3 cough classifier and estimates when its predictions should not be trusted, combining Monte Carlo Dropout, Temperature Scaling, and Mahalanobis distance in the embedding space. On a held-out split of 12,161 Coswara recordings, the probability-space methods are individually insufficient: Monte Carlo Dropout assigns lower uncertainty to incorrect than to correct predictions, and temperature scaling improves calibration but not discrimination. The strongest signal is embedding-space distance: Mahalanobis distance separates true positives from false positives (means 44.7 versus 63.4; Cohen's $d = 0.94$; AUROC 0.86) and, among predicted non-coughs, false negatives from true negatives (AUROC 0.82), so the same signal can flag both false alarms and missed coughs. At a descriptive operating point selected from labeled test data, retaining 90 percent of true-positive detections removes about two-thirds of false positives and raises precision from 59.9 to roughly 80 percent. A rejection threshold tuned on one split does not transfer to another (validation F1 of 82 percent falls to 34 percent on test), so we report threshold-independent AUROC as the main measure. An error taxonomy shows that failures are systematic, with most false positives related to speech. The pipeline runs on Raspberry Pi hardware costing 30 to 90 US dollars. Probabilistic calibration alone is thus inadequate for reliable cough detection.

Title: How Data Scales in Agentic Reinforcement Learning: Laws and Synthesis Strategies

  • Authors: Bowei He, Yankai Chen, Xiaokun Zhang, Changjiang Han, Ye Yuan, Chong Li, Xue Liu
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23946
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.23946
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.23946]] How Data Scales in Agentic Reinforcement Learning: Laws and Synthesis Strategies(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23946)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Pretraining scaling laws treat training data as a one-dimensional quantity: a token count. Agentic reinforcement learning (RL) inherits this scalar abstraction, but modern synthesis pipelines now generate \textit{tasks}, \textit{environments}, and \textit{trajectories} independently and at very different costs, turning data into a structured design space. This raises a question pretraining never had to answer: given a synthesis budget, \textit{which axis} should be scaled? We address this question end-to-end with three tightly coupled contributions. First, we decompose agentic data into three cardinality axes (task, environment, trajectory), plus a reward-density control variable, and fit per-axis scaling relations on different large language models (LLMs) across math+code, SWE, web, and UI/tool application domains. Across all model sizes, model families, domains, and RL algorithms (PPO and GRPO) we measured, the scaling exponents order as environment $>$ task $>$ trajectory. When environment diversity is decomposed into world and environment-structure diversity, its advantage is mainly driven by the former, while the latter is just comparable to the task diversity. Second, we use the fitted laws as a common ruler to price synthesis methods, capturing each method's efficiency as a per-axis discount factor and identifying critical synthetic-to-real ratios at which collapse begins; these ratios differ by an order of magnitude across axes. Third, we cast budget allocation as a constrained optimization under our laws and discounts, deriving a Chinchilla-style compute-optimal recipe and validating it at held-out scale. The empirical results demonstrate that following the recipe yields up to $1.7\times$ compute efficiency over the dominant ``scale-the-trajectories'' practice.

Title: Parameter misspecification in physics-informed neural networks: A closed-form audit and its limits

  • Authors: David McShannon, Nicholas Dietrich
  • Subjects: cs.LG, math.NA, physics.comp-ph
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23948
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.23948
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.23948]] Parameter misspecification in physics-informed neural networks: A closed-form audit and its limits(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23948)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Physics-informed neural networks (PINNs) are trained to minimize the residual of a prescribed equation, so a low training loss does not indicate whether a coefficient in that equation was specified incorrectly. This study evaluates what can be inferred about the encoded parameter from the trained network alone. When the residual is linear in the parameter, written as $A - \nu B$, the least-squares estimate $\hat{\nu} = A^{\top}B/B^{\top}B$ is obtained from a single evaluation of the frozen network, without retraining or reference data. Its accuracy depends on how closely the network satisfies its own equation, quantified by a normalized residual $\rho$. Recovery error is associated with $\rho$ (log-log correlation 0.72 across 36 convection-diffusion networks), and empirical exponents of 0.63 to 1.20 across three equations decompose into one half plus a measurable angular term. For Burgers' equation, a single-model test based on a block-bootstrap standard error flagged 3 of 300 correctly specified networks (observed false-alarm rate 1%), all 2% misspecifications at a practical budget, and two thirds of 1% misspecifications after convergence. For the self-similar blowup rate of the Córdoba-Córdoba-Fontelos equation, where the rate also enters the architecture, the estimator recovers about half of the misspecification, whereas $\rho$ increases approximately quadratically and distinguishes a 0.2% error. When the incorrect rate enters both the equation and the architecture, errors of up to 2% were not detected at the precision reached. At practical budgets, the raw loss can be lower for the misspecified model, a scaling effect that $\rho$ does not share.

Consequently, the generated shapes are not guaranteed to be functionally valid, limiting their direct use in downstream applications such as simulation and fabrication. In this paper, we present a computational approach to functionalize AI-generated 3D shapes through interior geometry construction. Our key observations are that a part's functional role defines its interior geometry, and such geometry is often smooth, relatively simple, and closely related to the part's external shape. The resulting 3D shapes are functionally valid while preserving their AI-generated external appearance. We demonstrate our approach on a variety of AI-generated 3D shapes, including vessels, containers, and storage furniture, and fabricate selected examples for functional validation.

  • Abstract: Raman spectroscopy generates large volumes of unlabeled data that are difficult to exploit with conventional supervised learning.   • Authors: Lei Tian, Chunzheng Zhu, Kai Wu, Yifan Zhang, Haihua Yang
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24005
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.24005
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.24005]] PACE: Pareto-Adaptive Compression for Efficient Native MLLMs(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24005)
  • Keywords: transformer, large language model
  • Abstract: Native multimodal large language models (native MLLMs) jointly encode visual and linguistic sequences within a unified autoregressive Transformer, emerging as a promising paradigm for multimodal understanding. Unlike conventional ViT-based MLLMs relying on external vision encoders, ViT-free native MLLMs tokenize pixel patches directly, potentially suffering severe visual token redundancy. A pilot study confirms this: unlike ViT-based models that degrade under compression, ViT-free MLLMs tolerate aggressive compression with near-lossless or even improved performance. Yet existing compression methods target ViT-based settings, leaving native MLLMs, which stand to gain the most, unexplored. To close this gap, we introduce an analysis pipeline centered on token discriminability and cross-dimensional heterogeneous redundancy, revealing that (i) redundancy is distributed independently and heterogeneously along height and width axes, and (ii) early-layer visual tokens are indiscriminable and uniformly redundant. Guided by these findings, we propose PACE (Pareto-Adaptive Compression for Efficient Native MLLMs), the first visual token compression framework tailored for native MLLMs with Multimodal-RoPE. Leveraging dimensional decoupling of positional encoding, PACE decomposes attention into two orthogonal spatial saliency estimates along height and width, gated by a positional attention signal, yielding direction-aware importance with negligible overhead. It further casts token budget allocation as Pareto optimization over performance preservation, inference efficiency, and spatial coverage, jointly identifying optimal compression depths and retention ratios. Experiments show PACE maintains near-lossless performance at only 25%-50% visual token retention, even improving several benchmarks, while delivering over 2x FLOPs reduction.

Title: SPADE-KD: Peer-to-Peer Collaborative Learning under Heterogeneous Settings

  • Authors: Harikrishna Kuttivelil, Katia Obraczka
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24013
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.24013
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.24013]] SPADE-KD: Peer-to-Peer Collaborative Learning under Heterogeneous Settings(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24013)
  • Keywords: federate
  • Abstract: Peer-to-peer collaborative learning is an emerging distributed deep learning paradigm that eliminates the need for a central aggregator and disseminator, avoiding the single-point-of-failure and bottleneck issues they can bring. Many such approaches use parameter averaging, requiring clients to have homogeneous model architectures, whereas others use knowledge distillation as an alternative while leaning on shared public data or centralized control infrastructure. Many of these approaches also make a one-size-fits-all assumption, i.e., jointly training a single, globally optimal solution for all clients, when in reality, clients' underlying data distributions might differ such that a globally optimal solution may not be locally optimal for all clients. To enable collaborative learning in environments with both model architecture and data distribution heterogeneity, we present SPADE-KD -- a fully decentralized model distillation framework that optimizes collaboration towards personalized rather than global performance. SPADE-KD leverages two mechanisms -- adaptive distillation and utility-driven peer selection. Adaptive distillation modulates the strength of each incoming model transfer by the performance advantage of the incoming model compared to the native model measured by their task losses, such that a client will distill more aggressively from incoming models that outperform it on its own local data. Utility-driven peer selection uses the loss-informed utility derived from adaptive distillation to rank prospective partners, focusing on exchanges that measurably improve the local model while suppressing low-utility transfers. Using federated classification tasks, we demonstrate that SPADE-KD consistent outperforms naive decentralized distillation with lower loss and faster convergence and that its individual components are more effective together than independently.

Title: Dynamical Kernel Attention: Estimating the Present from a Record of the Past

  • Authors: Peter Racioppo
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24026
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.24026
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.24026]] Dynamical Kernel Attention: Estimating the Present from a Record of the Past(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24026)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Standard self-attention compares tokens by similarity but does not account for how much of a past state survives to the present. We introduce Dynamical Kernel Attention (DKA), which derives attention from robust estimation of a latent state evolving under stochastic dynamics. As a past state decays toward equilibrium under the dynamics, the information it carries about the present vanishes, bounding the total evidence a head can draw. Unlike recency biases, DKA can abstain when relevant evidence is weak and guarantees a bounded error when old tokens are discarded. DKA preserves the parallel structure and computational cost of standard attention. We present the model and its theory; empirical evaluation is deferred to a subsequent version.

Title: The Window Is in the Reasoning: When LLM Agents Decide to Follow Prompt Injections

  • Authors: Haoxuan Jia, Yang Liu, Bin Chong, Yingguang Yang, Yancheng Chen, Jiayu Liang, Qian Li, Hanning Lu, Kefu Xu, Hao Zheng, Chongyang Zhang, Yulin Huang, Jianshen Zhang, Yongzhi Qi, Yunuo Lin, Hao Peng
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24048
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.24048
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.24048]] The Window Is in the Reasoning: When LLM Agents Decide to Follow Prompt Injections(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24048)
  • Keywords: attack
  • Abstract: LLM agents that read untrusted content can be hijacked by prompt injections. Runtime monitors that try to catch a hijack before the agent acts, and benchmarks that score each task with a single run, share an assumption: whether an agent follows an injection is settled when the injection is read. We test this assumption with branching rollouts that replay an episode exactly up to the step at which the agent reads an injection and then resample the rest of the episode many times, splitting the outcome variance into a part fixed at read time and a part decided afterwards. Across four AgentDojo suites, a Qwen3 agent without thinking is largely decided at read time (0.91 of the outcome variance lies between states); with thinking on, this share drops to 0.62 (95% CI of the drop [-0.38, -0.22]), in the same direction for three attack templates and two success labels. How much is fixed at read time is a property of the model that the same measurement reveals, and thinking lowers the attack success rate of Qwen3 but raises it for two other models. Resampling from reasoning prefixes shows that the decision forms gradually as the agent reasons. Zero-shot chain-of-thought monitors built on cheaper models miss much of what is already decided, whereas stronger monitors, and a lookahead monitor that judges simulated next actions, come close to what the prefix determines. Finally, single-run labels of thinking agents disagree with a rerun up to 35% of the time, so benchmarks need repeated runs per task.

Title: Red-Teaming Multi-Agent Attacks for Loss-of-Control Risks

  • Authors: John Kitaoka, Thao Amelia Pham, Carlos Rafael Giudice, Jacob Merizian
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.MA
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24052
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.24052
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.24052]] Red-Teaming Multi-Agent Attacks for Loss-of-Control Risks(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24052)
  • Keywords: attack
  • Abstract: AI agents are growing more capable and persistent, and are being deployed in ever larger numbers. We demonstrate distributed attacks, in which different agents carry out separate attack fragments, and how their covertness evades frontier monitors when reading their trajectories individually. By turning worst-case coordinated attacks into reproducible red-team cases, our work provides a foundation for preventing locally benign agent actions from accumulating into system-level loss of control.

Title: Alliance of Stochastic Routing and Reinforcement Fusion for Multi-Model Reasoning

  • Authors: Selim Furkan Tekin, Ling Liu
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.MA
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24054
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.24054
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.24054]] Alliance of Stochastic Routing and Reinforcement Fusion for Multi-Model Reasoning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24054)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: This paper studies dynamic model routing under uncertainty in multimodal settings, where a system must select and potentially compose multiple models to answer a query. Standard reinforcement learning approaches for routing often suffer from premature convergence to suboptimal models due to noisy rewards and insufficient exploration. We propose a unified vision-language framework (VL-Scout) for efficient multimodal and multi-step reasoning with turn-based reinforcement fusion. First, VL-Scout combines Bayesian priors, policy gradient optimization, and sequential decision-making to enable structured exploration and adaptive inference. A context-dependent Dirichlet belief model allows for epistemic uncertainty over model quality and is updated online based on observed rewards and context-specific evidence, enabling Thompson-style exploration while serving as a prior for regularization. Second, a routing policy is trained via a KL-regularized REINFORCE objective by maximizing a reward-weighted evidence lower bound, where the policy acts as an amortized posterior, and the Dirichlet belief serves as a prior. Third, we introduce a fusion policy trained with Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) or Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR), which operates over intermediate model rationales and selects between two actions: CONTINUE or ANSWER, terminating with a generated output. We evaluate VL-Scout on four representative multi-modal benchmarks (MMMU, MMMU-Pro, OKVQA, and RealWorldQA), compared against 11 representative routing and policy optimization baselines. Our method provides consistent performance improvements while reducing premature policy collapse. These results highlight the importance of integrating uncertainty-aware priors with both amortized policy learning and sequential fusion for robust and efficient model selection in discrete action spaces

  • Abstract: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have achieved impressive performance in generating text conditioned on inputs from different modalities. Title: GLOW: General Linear Models Optimized with Ward's Method

  • Authors: Matt Higger
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24085
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.24085
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.24085]] GLOW: General Linear Models Optimized with Ward's Method(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24085)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Neuroimaging seeks brain regions whose imaging features correlate with variables of interest (e.g., diagnosis, age), usually via the General Linear Model (GLM). Region-Based Analysis dilutes or splits any effect that does not fill exactly one atlas region. Voxel-Based Analysis, with Cluster Extent Thresholding and Threshold-Free Cluster Enhancement built on it, fits a GLM per voxel and groups adjacent significant voxels. But neighboring voxels can share a statistic without sharing a GLM, which a per-voxel statistic cannot detect. GLOW (General Linear Models Optimized with Ward's method) instead builds a hierarchy of nested regions by greedily minimizing GLM error (provably a special case of Ward's clustering). Maximum-statistic (Westfall-Young) permutation controls family-wise error over all regions. Significance spreads to an effect's nested fragments and the regions containing it; a final pruning step seeks whole, pure effect extents. We plant synthetic effects of known extent and varying strength in Human Connectome Project (HCP) diffusion images and in white noise. Pruning delivers wholeness: GLOW returns a spatially consistent effect as one region on 57% of HCP trials, the best baseline on 35%; baselines recover comparable volume, but in many pure pieces. The cost is positive predictive value (PPV): GLOW is more sensitive (by up to 0.29), but its regions overrun the effect, mostly because no hierarchy region isolates it, not because of pruning. On HCP, GLOW's mean Dice gains 0.059 over the best baseline at weaker effects and gives back 0.005 at stronger ones. On white noise, GLOW trails by up to 0.61 Dice: its hierarchy holds the effect, but its permutation test does not certify it. Runtime grows roughly linearly with voxel count, about 4.5 times the voxel-wise methods' on the full HCP volume. The PPV loss may trace to the assumption of spatially independent residuals.

  • Abstract: Bitstream-corrupted image restoration (BCIR) aims to reconstruct faithful images from corrupted compressed bitstreams. To address this challenge, we propose Bitstream Native Restorer (BiNar), a generative restoration framework that leverages fine-grained bitstream-native semantics to guide bitstream-corrupted image restoration. Finally, Blind Corruption Localization (BCoL) exploits the segmentation prior of visual foundation models to identify and locate error regions that requires recovery in corrupted images. The located error regions and the fine-grained descriptions jointly serve as visual prompt and language prompt of diffusion models image restoration, respectively. Extensive experiments under diverse bitstream corruption conditions show that BiNar outperforms all State-of-the-arts image restoration models regardless of perceptual quality or objective quality. Ablation studies demonstrate the effectiveness of fine-grained bitstream-native semantics in BCIR task.

Title: Hierarchical and Query-Aware Visual Token Pruning for High-Resolution LVLMs

  • Authors: Luther Wei, Ling Zou, Kang-Yang Huang, Yu-Syuan Xu, Wen-Huang Cheng
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24102
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.24102

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: High-resolution Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs) preserve critical, fine-grained visual details by encoding a global thumbnail alongside multiple local crops. However, the resulting thousands of vision tokens introduce severe computational bottlenecks during Large Language Model (LLM) inference. Existing token pruning methods rely primarily on query-agnostic visual saliency, while current query-aware approaches either operate at a coarse crop-level resolution or compute token relevance too late, after vision tokens have already entered the heavy LLM layers. To address this, we propose a training-free, hierarchical token pruning framework that utilizes query guidance prior to executing any LLM blocks. Our top-down hierarchy first operates at the instance-level, dynamically scaling the vision token retention ratio of each sample based on its visual complexity. Next, at the crop-level, we distribute this sample budget across local crops using sentence-level cross-modal similarity. Finally, at the token-level, we exploit projected vision tokens alongside textual query token embeddings in the shared space to measure fine-grained alignment, applying a text-debiasing normalization to produce high-fidelity query scores. These query-guided budgets and scores are fused with local and global visual saliency metrics to select the most critical vision tokens. Extensive experiments on LLaVA-NeXT-7B demonstrate that our plug-and-play framework retains 98.0% and 94.8% of unpruned performance at 25% and 10% vision token retention, respectively. Notably, at 25% retention, our method delivers a 2.0x inference throughput speedup and even surpasses the unpruned baseline on POPE by 0.7 points.

Title: Prefix-Tuning for Arbitrary Output Sequences on Pretrained Transformers

  • Authors: Peter Cho-Ho Lam, Ziyi Wang, Zirui Zhou
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24123
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.24123
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.24123]] Prefix-Tuning for Arbitrary Output Sequences on Pretrained Transformers(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24123)
  • Keywords: fair, transformer, large language model
  • Abstract: Prefix-tuning is a parameter-efficient fine-tuning method for large language models (LLMs) that steers model outputs by prepending task-specific prefix representations to the input sequence. Despite its practical success, the theoretical understanding of the expressiveness of prefixes remains incomplete. In particular, the following question remains open: Given a pretrained autoregressive Transformer, for any input-output sequence pair $(\mathtt{x},\mathtt{y})$, does there always exist a prefix $\mathcal{S}$ such that prepending $\mathcal{S}$ to $\mathtt{x}$ induces the model to generate the target output $\mathtt{y}$? In this work, we prove that under the assumption that $\mathtt{y}$ is in the range of the pretrained Transformer, such a prefix $\mathcal{S}$ exists and can be explicitly computed by solving multiple linear systems. We further show that this assumption is necessary, in the sense that such a prefix may not exist when the assumption fails, thereby providing a complete answer to the above question. Our analysis is constructive and the obtained theoretical results hold under fairly general assumptions on model architecture. Extensive experiments on GPT-2 and Qwen3-4B demonstrate that our algorithm produces prefixes with strong control over model outputs.

Title: Recording-Aware Evaluation of Multimodal OT Intrusion Detection

  • Keywords: attack
  • Abstract: Machine-learning-based intrusion detection systems (IDSs) for operational technology (OT) are commonly evaluated on benchmark datasets in which normal and attack data are collected in separate recordings. In such settings, high performance may reflect recording-associated differences as well as attack behavior. We introduce a recording-aware evaluation procedure that compares attack windows with background from the same recording, uses prior data for model fitting and calibration, tests temporal alignment against randomized event schedules, and reports event coverage together with alarm burden. Title: Inside the Loop: A Mechanistic Study of Weight-Tied Transformers on Depth-Bound Algorithmic Tasks

  • Authors: Magnus Sesodia, Juil Sock, Philip Torr, Christian Schroeder de Witt
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24135
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.24135
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.24135]] Inside the Loop: A Mechanistic Study of Weight-Tied Transformers on Depth-Bound Algorithmic Tasks(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24135)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Solving algorithmic tasks with many sequential steps typically requires scaling up transformer depth or chain-of-thought length, incurring unfavorable parameter and inference-compute costs, respectively. Looped transformers, which reuse one block of layers across many iterations, are poised to address this, since they scale effective depth without adding parameters or tokens. Yet, it remains unclear what each iteration is actually computing, or whether the loop merely simulates a deeper untied stack. Here, we study directed permutation traversal, in which a model follows $H$ hops from a starting node and reports the node it reaches, so every intermediate hop has a known ground truth. We find that a minimum effective depth is necessary to solve the task, beyond which the degree of weight tying becomes the determining factor. For instance, a looped transformer with a four-layer block iterated eight times solves the task in every seed, while an identically-trained untied transformer of the same effective depth never does, succeeding only with a lower learning rate or supervision of its intermediate layers. Probing and intervening on the residual stream, we find that the looped model advances through the intermediate nodes gradually, forming a staircase that adapts to its iteration budget, whereas the untied model, at that lower rate, exposes them only in its final layers. Taken together, our results recast looped transformers as iterative program learners, which allocate their iterations to successive steps of the algorithm and solve tasks that identically-trained untied models of the same effective depth do not. Code is available at this https URL.

Title: Continual Test-Time Learning with Evolution Strategies

  • Authors: Guangya Hao, Yiting Duan, Longyan Tan, Yunbo Long, Hao Wu, Ge Zheng, Alexandra Brintrup, Zhuokai Zhao
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24137
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.24137
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.24137]] Continual Test-Time Learning with Evolution Strategies(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24137)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models increasingly operate in open-ended environments, where they are expected to continually adapt to evolving tasks and distributions, often without access to external supervision. Existing continual learning approaches rely on external supervision, such as labeled data, demonstrations, or reward signals, which can be costly or unavailable in practice. Test-time adaptation removes this dependence by learning from self-generated supervision, but existing methods derive that supervision from the single policy being updated, so once the policy drifts, its own learning signal drifts with it, leading to policy collapse, degraded reasoning, and catastrophic forgetting. In this paper, we propose \textbf{Evolution Strategy Reinforcement Learning (ESRL)}, a continual test-time learning framework that draws its self-generated supervision from a population of policies instead of the single policy being trained. ESRL perturbs an anchor policy in parameter space to form a population of nearby policies, samples one trajectory from each, and aggregates their answers into label-free group-relative learning signals for the central student. The anchor is held fixed within each block and refreshed from the student at block boundaries, so the population stays centered on a stable yet current policy and continues to supply diverse and reliable supervision as the student moves. In single-task test-time adaptation across mathematical reasoning, general knowledge, and medical domains, ESRL improves average accuracy by up to 31.7% relative to the base model and 16.6% relative to the strongest test-time adaptation baseline. Notably, ESRL substantially reduces catastrophic forgetting and achieves the strongest final performance in continual learning.

Title: Preservation and loss of algorithmic structure in experimental diffusion of joint-action norms

  • Authors: Dhara Yu, Bill D. Thompson

  • Abstract: Everyday norms of social interaction - e.g. norms of sharing space, taking turns, or queuing - are essential to large societies, offering structured procedures for common interactions. Title: On Reparameterizing the Score Function

  • Authors: Mohammad Hossein Amani, Leonardo Bocchieri, Maxime Peyrard, Clément Hongler, Robert West
  • Subjects: cs.LG, stat.ML
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24188
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.24188
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.24188]] On Reparameterizing the Score Function(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24188)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Policy gradients for autoregressive sequence models differentiate each token's log-probability while holding the conditioning history fixed. This discards information about how earlier decisions shape later conditional distributions, creating a credit-assignment bottleneck when rewards are sparse. We introduce History-Reparameterized (HR) policy gradients, which replace the frozen prefix in the score function with a differentiable reparameterized or relaxed history, allowing gradients of downstream log-probabilities to propagate backward through the generated trajectory. The resulting history-pathwise term is generally biased when added directly, so we derive Optimal HR (OHR), a centered control-variate estimator with a closed-form coefficient that minimizes the trace of the gradient variance at the population level. The variance reduction is governed by temporal structure in the policy dynamics: random or weakly structured policies provide little benefit, while policies with learned sequential dependencies yield informative history-pathwise corrections. For discrete tokens, the choice of relaxation is crucial: embedding-level relaxation yields substantial variance reduction that grows with horizon, whereas straight-through Gumbel-Softmax yields essentially none in our diagnostics. We validate the method on a Gaussian linear Markov model, a sparse-reward T-maze with a recurrent policy, and an autoregressive Transformer model for small Traveling Salesman Problem (TSP) instances.

Title: SPIDES: Information-Flow Control for Browser Agents

  • Authors: Egor Zverev, Juhee Kim, Leon Trampert, Boris Köpf, Aashish Kolluri, Shruti Tople, Santiago Zanella-Béguelin
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24277

  • Copy Paste: [[2610.24277]] SPIDES: Information-Flow Control for Browser Agents(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24277)
  • Keywords: security, protect, defense, attack
  • Abstract: Browser agents automate complex workflows by reading and acting on arbitrary page content, but this flexibility exposes them to prompt injection attacks and unintended data exfiltration. Recent work argues that LLM-driven browser agents erode traditional web security boundaries such as the Same-Origin Policy (SOP) by acting across origins with the user's authority and no built-in isolation. We argue that restoring SOP is neither sufficient (a same-origin public sink can leak private data) nor necessary (legitimate tasks require authorized cross-origin flows) for protecting the security of users. We propose SPIDES, which instead enforces information-flow control by gating primitive browser actions such as typing, clicking and navigating on labels propagated from element-level HTML confidentiality and integrity annotations. To evaluate SPIDES and baseline defenses under a common threat model, we build MicroNet, a closed ecosystem of 28 interlinked websites with element-level ground-truth labels and paired benign/adversarial tasks. SPIDES achieves 0% attack success rate by design while retaining high task completion rate and autonomy. Our findings demonstrate that information-flow control is an effective technique to extend web security to browser agents, gaining on autonomy and utility against state-of-the-art baselines. The source code and data are openly accessible at this https URL.

Title: Dataset Inference for Data Provenance and Privacy Auditing in Tabular Foundation Models

  • Authors: Dariush Wahdany (1), Jesse Cresswell (2), Naiqing Guan (2), Atiyeh Ashari Ghomi (2), Franziska Boenisch (1), Adam Dziedzic (1) ((1) CISPA Helmholtz Center for Information Security, (2) Layer 6 AI)

  • Keywords: privacy
  • Abstract: Tabular foundation models (TFMs) are increasingly deployed through black-box APIs and trained on large collections of real-world tabular datasets. Title: Empirical Analysis of TEE-Enabled Rollup Designs

  • Authors: Claudiu-Nicu Bărbieru, Corentin Libert, Peterson Yuhala, Andrei Arusoaie, Matthieu Bettinger, Oana-Otilia Captarencu, Pascal Felber, Emanuel Onica, Etienne Rivière, Valerio Schiavoni
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.DC
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24284
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.24284

  • Keywords: security
  • Abstract: Layer 2 (L2) rollups have become the dominant scaling solution in Ethereum. At the core of these systems lies the rollup sequencer, a component that orders, executes, and submits L2 transaction blocks to the main Ethereum blockchain. The centralization of this sequencer introduces security risks, including censorship and malicious manipulation of transactions to extract maximum extractable value (MEV). Trusted execution environments (TEEs) provide strong hardware-enforced confidentiality and integrity guarantees for code and data. This work addresses this gap through a comprehensive empirical study of TEE-enabled rollup designs under both PBS and monolithic sequencer models. Our experimental evaluations show that the impact of TEEs on rollup performance is small and that PBS does not significantly amplify it. We also show that PBS can lead to significant performance improvements by enabling more specialized, efficient builder logic implementations.

  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Pixel-based language models process rendered text without relying on a fixed subword vocabulary, offering a promising approach to noisy and multilingual inputs.   • Keywords: security, protect
  • Abstract: AI agents increasingly interact with external environments to complete complex user tasks. These interactions can lead to unexpected behavior and expose agents to security threats such as indirect prompt injection. Current agent security systems largely protect agents by restricting their actions, which can result in a substantial security-utility trade-off for complex, open-ended tasks. To address this, we propose a quantitative risk-based approach to access control for AI agents that regulates measurable risks accumulated during task execution. As a concrete realization of this approach, we introduce RATION, a security framework that tracks measurable risks associated with agent actions and enforces quantitative constraints throughout execution. Title: Defending Against Dormant Poisoning Attacks Across Language and Multimodal Agents

  • Authors: Wonjun Lee, Vaidehi Patil, Zaid Khan, Yue Zhang, Hyunji Lee, Suhyun Kim, Mohit Bansal
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.CL, cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24379
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.24379
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.24379]] Defending Against Dormant Poisoning Attacks Across Language and Multimodal Agents(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24379)
  • Keywords: defense, attack
  • Abstract: Recent work shows that attackers can implant dormant malicious behaviors that remain hidden at release but emerge after benign fine-tuning. As foundation models are used as agents, whether dormant poisoning extends to harmful agent actions and multimodal settings remains unclear. We show two agentic risks: (1) harmful behaviors activated by benign fine-tuning can extend to unseen actions; (2) multiple harmful behaviors can coexist and be jointly activated by benign downstream fine-tuning. Existing defenses often rely on external safe responses, which are hard to obtain for agent actions, or a trusted clean reference model. We exploit a key property of dormant poisoning: the attacker must preserve safe behavior at release to conceal poisoning, and this behavior can serve as a safety reference. Leveraging this property, we automatically construct a Safety Buffer pairing harmful inputs with the released model's responses and benign versions generated by the same model to remove harmful intent. The Safety Buffer can replace external safe responses in existing defenses, but these defenses do not distinguish updates that weaken safety from those that preserve it, often increasing over-refusal or reducing utility. To address this limitation, we introduce LookAhead Defense, which previews benign fine-tuning updates using the Safety Buffer and penalizes only updates predicted to weaken safe behavior on harmful inputs or increase the likelihood of refusals on benign versions in the Safety Buffer. Across language models, language agents, and multimodal agents, LookAhead Defense reduces harmful behavior in agents from up to 93.9% to at most 14.2% and harmful responses in language models from up to 92.5% to at most 2.0% versus vanilla fine-tuning, while maintaining competitive utility and low over-refusal. It remains effective against other poisoning methods.
