We hypothesize that the CNN layers can be replaced by a simple, universal model. To test this hypothesis, we learn a two-layer feature extractor via distillation from one or more teacher models (HuBERT, data2vec, and WavLM). Experiments on SUPERB(-SG) benchmark tasks show that our model largely retains the performance of the teacher models and can also be used in an unseen model (wav2vec 2.0). Our feature extractor requires only 3% of the MACs of the CNNs, resulting in a $>$20% overall inference runtime reduction.

Title: Transformers as Adaptive Kernel Learners across Tasks for In-Context Learning

  • Authors: Zhaiming Shen, Alexander Cloninger, Rongjie Lai, Wenjing Liao
  • Subjects: cs.LG, math.NA, math.ST
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24441
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.24441
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.24441]] Transformers as Adaptive Kernel Learners across Tasks for In-Context Learning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24441)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Transformers have emerged as a dominant architecture in modern machine learning, with in-context learning (ICL) enabling models to adapt to new tasks by conditioning on prompts, without any parameter updates. To understand the attention mechanism in transformer blocks, a growing line of theoretical work interprets ICL through the lens of classical statistical estimators. Recent works have established a connection between the attention mechanism and kernel methods, while the kernel function is usually assumed to be fixed. In practice, the query and key matrices in the attention mechanism are trained, which allows the attention mechanism to serve as an adaptive kernel method. In this work, we demonstrate that transformers can act as adaptive anisotropic kernel learners. Specifically, we study ICL for low-rank linear and multi-index nonlinear regressions, and conduct numerical simulations to show that the learned attention scores closely resemble the oracle kernels for these function classes. We then develop a general framework to analyze the generalization error of ICL by connecting transformers to the oracle kernel estimator for each function class. Our theory gives an explicit and constructive implementation of the oracle kernel estimators for these function classes using a wide and shallow transformer architecture, illustrating how the prediction is carried out by the interaction between the query token and the context tokens. Our work complements existing studies by providing numerical and theoretical evidence of how the attention mechanism adapts to anisotropic kernels in both linear and nonlinear settings.

Title: Generative Retrieval for Reverse Target Fishing with a Proteome-Scale Benchmark

  • Abstract: Reverse target fishing identifies protein targets for a query molecule and is central to drug discovery and repurposing. We reformulate the task as generative retrieval and introduce GenFish. Residual vector quantization converts frozen protein language model features into shared semantic identifiers. A controlled positive-hiding experiment shows that targeted removal of false-negative competition improves contrastive retrieval beyond matched random masking; GenFish remains stronger under the same incomplete supervision. Pfam alignment indicates that the semantic identifiers capture biologically meaningful relationships among proteins. These results establish generative retrieval as a principled paradigm for reverse target fishing under incomplete bioactivity records.

Title: Measuring Empowerment from Offline Data for Intrinsically Motivated Exploration

  • Authors: Ishir Garg, Vivek Myers, Qiyang Li, Catherine Ji, Benjamin Eysenbach, Sergey Levine
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24461
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.24461
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.24461]] Measuring Empowerment from Offline Data for Intrinsically Motivated Exploration(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24461)
  • Keywords: extraction
  • Abstract: Large, unlabeled datasets of prior experience have become increasingly available for agentic tasks in domains such as robotics and coding. A common recipe for these tasks is to train a policy using the prior data, and then further improve it with online rollouts. But while much work has studied how to use both the offline and online data for policy extraction, far less work has been done to understand how the prior data can be used to efficiently \textit{explore} during the online learning phase. Previous work has attempted to formalize this idea by defining the \textit{empowerment}, a measure of how many future states an agent can realize; states with higher empowerment require more exploration as there are more futures to explore. However, existing methods for estimating empowerment require online policy rollouts in the environment. We propose the first method for estimating empowerment from unlabeled prior data, without any environment interaction. By jointly learning an empowerment-maximizing policy together with its discounted state-occupancy measure, our estimator can be trained on unlabeled trajectories generated from any unknown policy. We demonstrate that our empowerment estimator qualitatively gives intuitive empowerment measures and show that the pretrained estimator can be used to improve exploration in the online goal-conditioned reinforcement learning setting. Code is available at \url{this https URL}.

Title: TideKV: Delayed KV Eviction via Layer-Wise Chunked Prefill for Memory-Efficient Long-Context LLM Inference

  • Authors: Yu Wang, Zhu Wang, Ning Hu, Yifan Guo, Xiu Ma, Bin Guo, Zhiwen Yu
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.DC
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24499
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.24499
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.24499]] TideKV: Delayed KV Eviction via Layer-Wise Chunked Prefill for Memory-Efficient Long-Context LLM Inference(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24499)
  • Keywords: transformer, large language model
  • Abstract: Long-context large language model (LLM) inference is important for modern intelligent applications but remains constrained by peak memory during prompt prefill. Existing KV cache compression reduces decoding memory but typically requires full-prompt prefill to preserve global context, retaining a high construction peak. Online chunk-level eviction lowers memory earlier but selects before each layer has observed the complete prompt, potentially degrading cache construction and decoding. We present TideKV, an on-GPU cache-construction architecture using layer-major delayed eviction. TideKV processes all prompt chunks within each Transformer layer, retains full-prompt KV only for the active layer, and evicts at layer end. This schedule preserves full-prompt-aware selection while reducing activation and temporary memory. Experiments across multiple computing platforms show that TideKV preserves the inference quality and final cache size of the evaluated compression methods. At 64K on A100, it reduces the construction-time transient footprint by 83.89%. On RTX 4090, it increases time to first token by only 1.06% at 64K and enables 128K prefill. TideKV also completes 128K on Jetson, whereas matched post-prefill compression runs out of memory on both resource-constrained platforms. These results show that delayed layer-end eviction substantially expands feasible context lengths without sacrificing cache utility.

LVLMs consistently favor longer options even when visually inconsistent, with accuracy dropping 2--38\% when correct answers are shorter and increasing 1--70\% when longer. This bias persists across model scales. Blurry-image experiments show that bias intensifies when visual evidence weakens, while abstention tests reveal that LVLMs struggle to refuse incorrect predictions. These results underscore that inference-time interventions are insufficient, pointing to the need for architectural solutions and bias-aware training.

We investigate whether explicitly structuring latent state evolution can improve the stability and utility of such imagined trajectories. To this end, we introduce HWM, a structured dynamics framework that represents backbone latents in a learned phase space consisting of generalized coordinates and momenta. Future states are propagated through dissipative Hamiltonian dynamics and mapped back to the original latent representation through a learnable inverse interface. The resulting formulation can be integrated into existing world-model architectures without redesigning their perception or decoding components, while multi-step transition supervision encourages the structured rollout to remain aligned with the latent trajectories induced by real observations. Controlled simulation experiments show that HWM produces more accurate estimates of velocity, acceleration, and jerk and substantially reduces long-horizon energy drift relative to unstructured latent dynamics. We further evaluate the approach across three distinct embodied world-model settings, covering single-arm multi-view prediction with Ctrl-World, dual-arm single-view prediction with EVAC, and task-oriented single-arm prediction with Cosmos-Predict2.5-2B. On RoboCasa, incorporating HWM into Cosmos-Predict2.5-2B yields a 2.7% relative improvement in task success. On real-world AgiBot World trajectories, it improves nDTW by 9.7% with Ctrl-World and Logic by 8.4% with EVAC.

  • Keywords: federate
  • Abstract: Federated learning has recently emerged as an important paradigm for large-scale distributed model training, which can be naturally formulated as a class of distributed learning problems. However, its practical performance is often hindered by significant communication overhead and system heterogeneity.   • Abstract: We study feature vectors: directions in a language model's activation space that correspond to human-interpretable concepts, such as honesty or deception. Title: AdamPM: Memory-Efficient LLM Optimization via Moment Pooling

  • Authors: Xiaowei Ji, Lishan Xu, Jianing An, Jie Luo, Wenjun Wu, Lei Huang
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24598

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Adam is widely used for training large language models (LLMs) owing to its strong optimization performance and training stability, but its element-wise estimation for the first and second moments incur substantial memory overhead. Although numerous memory-efficient optimizers have been proposed, many incur additional computational overhead or require additional tuning of hyperparameters. Consequently, Adam remains a dominant and reliable choice for LLM pre-training. We propose AdamPM (Adam with Pooled Moments), a simple and memory-efficient variant of Adam that groups adjacent moment entries along the row or column dimension and represents each group by a single shared mean. AdamPM can well inherit Adam's hyperparameter settings and is readily integrated into existing Adam optimizer implementations, with less memory overhead. We systematically investigate pooling of both first and second moment estimates and identify second moment pooling as the effective design. With a group size of $s$, AdamPM reduces second moment memory to $1/s$ of the original requirement. Extensive experiments on language model pretraining, fine-tuning and multimodal learning demonstrate that AdamPM maintains model performance and training speed while substantially reducing optimizer-state memory. Our code is available at \url{this https URL}.

To address this, we analyze cross-lingual safety behavior at the neuron level and identify a compact set of safety-related neurons shared across languages, termed the safety kernel (SK).   • Abstract: Bug bounty platforms receive vulnerability reports faster than vendors can triage them, and Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly considered for assigning the Common Vulnerability Scoring System (CVSS) severity that drives prioritization. Errors are directional: models overestimate far more often than they underestimate, and they almost never recognize low-severity vulnerabilities. We trace these errors to three semantic boundaries that the specification draws and narrative reports blur: potential versus realized impact, impact magnitude versus authorization scope, and uncertainty versus the worst case. Because the violations originate partly in the report, we close with an exploratory case study: on four reports, a boundary-aligned, exploit-free report structure lowered a frontier model's score where the narrative invited an unclaimed account takeover, but left the metrics a report does not settle open, and the two published labels disagreed on three of the four reports.

Title: PV-Decoding: Progressive Verification Decoding with Decode Gating for Diffusion Language Models

  • Authors: Renxi Liu, Yiming Li, Shengyin Sun, Chen Chen, Xian Wang, Shuai Yang, Huiling Zhen, Xianzhi Yu, Yaoyuan Wang, Weizhe Lin, Xiaosong Li
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24722
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.24722
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.24722]] PV-Decoding: Progressive Verification Decoding with Decode Gating for Diffusion Language Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24722)
  • Keywords: robust, diffusion
  • Abstract: Discrete diffusion language models (dLLMs) offer an attractive alternative to autoregressive generation by updating multiple tokens in parallel, but their inference efficiency is still limited by the cost of safely committing tentative predictions. In particular, speculative decoding for dLLMs is challenging because speculative tokens within the same diffusion block may depend on one another, making aggressive acceptance prone to error propagation and strict verification computationally expensive. We present PV-Decoding, a confidence-driven speculative decoding framework with progressive verification for block diffusion inference. PV-Decoding partitions masked positions into directly decoded tokens, speculative tokens, and unresolved tokens using two confidence thresholds, and then verifies speculative tokens with progressively stronger policies, including single-batch, dual-batch, and multi-batch verification. This design exposes a practical accuracy--efficiency trade-off: weaker verification minimizes overhead, whereas stronger verification improves acceptance precision and robustness. Experiments on three diffusion language models across GSM8K, MBPP, and HumanEval show that PV-Decoding reduces sequential model forward passes (MFPs) by 8%--37% relative to confidence-based decoding, while keeping the largest observed accuracy drop to 1.3%. On Dream-7B-Instruct/GSM8K, PV-Decoding (1-batch) and PV-Decoding (2-batch) improve throughput by 7.0%--13.6% under the same hardware budget, whereas PV-Decoding (multi-batch) is slower under the current runtime. These results suggest that confidence-guided speculation with structured verification is a practical path toward efficient and reliable dLLM inference.

Title: How Does Muon Work in Private Deep Learning? Scalable Differentially Private Training via Orthogonalization

  • Authors: Alexander Gaponov, Grigory Malinovsky, Egor Shulgin, Peter Richtarik
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24736
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.24736

  • Keywords: privacy
  • Abstract: Training deep neural networks requires optimizers that balance computational efficiency with formal data privacy. While orthogonalized update rules, such as the recently introduced Muon optimizer, have shown strong empirical performance and scalability, their behavior under Differential Privacy (DP) is not yet understood. In this paper, we propose DP-Muon, a differentially private variant of the Muon optimizer. By analyzing Muon's orthogonalized updates through the lens of a Linear Minimization Oracle (LMO), we show that the leading effect of per-example clipping is a positive rescaling of the gradient, to which the LMO is blind, so that only a small directional residual controlled by the centered gradient variance enters the optimization error. This yields an $\mathcal O(K^{-1/4})$ convergence rate, up to logarithmic factors, to a clipping-dependent neighborhood at a fixed noise scale and, with noise calibrated to a fixed $(\varepsilon,\delta)$ budget, the standard $(\sqrt d/(n\varepsilon))^{1/2}$ privacy--utility bound of DP-SGD, without assuming bounded gradients. Furthermore, we investigate the hyperparameter landscape of DP-Muon and document how the optimal learning rate, momentum, and batch size change under privacy. Empirically, on image classification DP-Muon matches or improves on DP-SGD and DP-Adam, whereas on language modeling the best optimizer depends on the batch size, with DP-Muon best at small batches and DP-Adam best near the privacy-optimal batch size.

Title: CorrCLIPv2: Harnessing Unlabeled Images for Open-Vocabulary Semantic Segmentation

  • Abstract: Open-vocabulary semantic segmentation aims to classify pixels without being restricted to a fixed set of categories. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our approach establishes a new state-of-the-art on 10 standard benchmarks. Title: UniAVC: Unified Audio-Video Creation via Progressive Training and Asymmetric Scheduling

  • Authors: Kien T. Pham, Yuhao Liu, Chong Hou Choi, Zhenqi He, Yanghao Wang, Chenyang Lei, Rui Liu, Qifeng Chen, Long Chen

  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24791
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.24791
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.24791]] UniAVC: Unified Audio-Video Creation via Progressive Training and Asymmetric Scheduling(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24791)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: Unified audio-video creation requires a single model capable of text-conditioned joint synthesis, bidirectional cross-modal generation, and instruction-guided editing across both modalities. Existing methods rely heavily on paired corpora, which are scarce for joint generation and even rarer for editing. Consequently, they are spent learning single-branch generative and editing capabilities that abundant unimodal data could readily teach, rather than on the cross-modal coupling. Furthermore, prevailing systems apply a single shared noise schedule to both modalities, neglecting two critical limitations. First, due to the significant discrepancy in information density, video requires prolonged high noise to organize spatial and motion structure, whereas the sparser audio signal can settle earlier during the synthesis process, the dynamics a uniform schedule might compromise. Modality-decoupled schedulers meanwhile warp the noise schedule independently from a shared timestep, while subtasks with one clean modality directly supervise asymmetric configurations. These settings respectively enable intent-aware editing, which preserves one modality intact while enlisting it to constrain the other, and post-inference self-refinement, which regenerates only an unsatisfactory branch against an accepted one. UniAVC thus spans multiple creation tasks from a single set of weights without task-specific adapters.

Title: Who Gets Unmasked? Sampler-Induced Bias in Diffusion Language Models

  • Authors: Xujun Che, Farsheed Haque, Depeng Xu, Xi Niu, Haopeng Zhang
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24795
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.24795
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.24795]] Who Gets Unmasked? Sampler-Induced Bias in Diffusion Language Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24795)
  • Keywords: fair, diffusion
  • Abstract: Diffusion language models (dLLMs) generate by gradually unmasking a canvas, so their samplers decide not only which token to write but also when to commit it. We show that this second decision changes who appears in generated text, a sampler-induced bias on top of the model's own. Common commit rules, including LLaDA's default, commit the positions whose sampled tokens are most probable; this peeking favors majority options. To separate the sampler from the model, we read the model's belief about a fork, such as a protagonist's gender, in one forward pass, and show that this belief matches exact sampling from the model. Measured against it, peeking samplers on LLaDA and Dream amplify occupational gender stereotypes and collapse opinion distributions, and confidence-based ordering shifts gender-balanced occupations toward men, more so under faster parallel decoding. We propose Fork-First Sampling, which decides the fork before anything else is written: it follows the belief or any target distribution, improves fluency, and avoids the stories that remark on the protagonist's gender, which prompt-based fixes produce, at the cost of one forward pass. Fairness evaluations of dLLMs should report the commit rule.

Title: From Trust Scores to Driver Decisions: An Abstaining Evidential Decision Layer for Event Validation in VANETs

  • Authors: İpek Abasıkeleş Turgut
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.NI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24810
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.24810
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.24810]] From Trust Scores to Driver Decisions: An Abstaining Evidential Decision Layer for Event Validation in VANETs(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24810)
  • Keywords: attack
  • Abstract: Trust management in vehicular ad hoc networks is highly active, yet the decision that a driver needs, whether a reported event is real, has received far less attention; the schemes that make it force every decision into two outcomes, even on thin or conflicting evidence. This paper presents CREST, a vehicle-side event-decision framework that can withhold a decision its evidence does not support. Each vehicle weights the reports it holds by the trust, proximity and freshness of their senders, fuses them with Yager's rule, and decides only when the fused margin reaches an abstention threshold. Abstention is a waiting state: a vehicle still unsure at the event is resolved by an explicit application policy, which makes the trade-off between missed hazards and unnecessary detours visible. CREST requires only a scalar trust per reporter and is built here on an evidential trust model whose consensus and revocation steps are updated to support the decision layer. A single report is shown to change the decision margin by at most its weight, which bounds the influence of an attacker coalition by its trust. In a pre-registered simulation study with fourteen attacks, including position spoofing and trust-gated camouflage, and with all unspecified parameters of all systems tuned by the same procedure, CREST reduces the decision error of two published trust-based dissemination systems from 0.162 and 0.106 to 0.051. With the best-guess policy it also lowers both missed and unnecessary reroutes and outperforms the forced binary rule on both error types in every cell.

  • Abstract: On-device fine-tuning lets a large language model (LLM) learn from its user's private data without uploading it. A phone's neural processing unit (NPU) already holds these weights for inference; using it for training also requires computing input gradients, which its inference interface does not support. Its runtime fuses the remaining CPU operators and overlaps NPU execution with adapter computation, while resumable execution and thermal pacing let training proceed as a background job. We evaluate it on two Qualcomm Snapdragon platforms using four LLMs from the Qwen and Llama families. Our workloads cover nightly personalization with labels supplied on the phone and the public LaMP-2 benchmark; comparisons include an optimized CPU trainer, a phone-GPU trainer, forward-only training, and GPU-trained references.

  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24823

  • Keywords: attack, large language model
  • Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM) agents are vulnerable to adversarial inputs that induce attacker-specified malicious behaviors, commonly referred to as jailbreaking. Existing optimization-based jailbreak attacks primarily employ first-order (FO) methods that leverage gradient information or zeroth-order (ZO) methods that rely on function-value feedback. However, their complementarity and optimization ordering remain underexplored. We systematically investigate four strategies: pure ZO, pure FO, ZO->FO, and FO->ZO, and develop a ZO-FO framework that explores adversarial prefixes through ZO optimization and refines their corresponding suffixes through FO optimization. To coordinate the two processes, we introduce Pair-aware optimization, which retains multiple prefix candidates, conditionally refines their suffixes and selects among the resulting prefix-suffix pairs. We further investigate agent jailbreaking from a more general stochastic-generation perspective, where the same adversarial input can induce diverse reasoning trajectories and actions that a single greedy output cannot capture. Accordingly, we adopt consistent stochastic decoding during both optimization and evaluation. Experiments across malicious-instruction and malicious-environment benchmarks demonstrate that hybrid optimization improves attack effectiveness over single-optimizer methods, while optimization order substantially influences performance.

Title: MisleadQA: Diagnosing LLM Failures under Misleading Context

  • Authors: Hui Wang, Bohao Chu, Qianli Wang, Shuning Zhang, Norbert Fuhr
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24851

  • Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are widely used to answer questions from retrieved or user-provided context. Prior work has mainly studied factually false or irrelevant context. It remains unclear whether such out-of-scope context misleads models as much as false context, and whether adding gold context prevents models from being misled by either. We introduce **MisleadQA**, a diagnostic dataset of multiple-choice questions with an abstention option. Each question pairs gold context (G) with true but out-of-scope context (S) and false but in-scope context (F) supporting the same wrong answer. We evaluate six open-weight LLMs. On questions they answer correctly without context, providing S alone leads them to switch to this wrong answer in 29.3% of cases, and providing F alone does so in 75.4%. Adding G nearly removes the effect of S, but F still misleads models in 21.0% of cases, especially when F comes first. A context-faithful instruction reduces these errors mainly through abstention and lowers accuracy even with G. These findings suggest that evaluations should check whether context applies to the question, not only whether it is true, and should report correct, misled, and abstention rates together. Title: LLM-Based Evaluation Planning for Heterogeneous Optimization

  • Authors: Nian Ran, Sebastian Brulin, Markus Olhofer, Mauricio A. Álvarez, Richard Allmendinger

  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24855

  • Abstract: Heterogeneous optimization problems require an optimizer to decide not only which candidate solutions to evaluate, but also which objectives and constraints should be evaluated under different costs and fidelities. In this work, we propose REP-HO, a reasoning-based evaluation planner that replaces the rule-based mixed-fidelity selection step with a large language model (LLM) planner. We first show that REP-HO achieves competitive performance compared to the human-designed metric-based method (MFE-NSGA-III). Super-resolution can recover higher-resolution images, but existing methods are typically specialised to one scale or acquisition geometry, limiting their use across heterogeneous clinical protocols. We present GSMR3D, a continuous volumetric model that reconstructs arbitrary isotropic and anisotropic resolutions with a single checkpoint per MRI contrast. It combines a learned dictionary of anisotropic 3D Gaussian prototypes with scale-conditioned frequency encoding and an exact measurement-consistency projection, keeping reconstructions consistent with the acquired low-resolution data. We train separate models on 3,731 contrast-enhanced T1-weighted and 30,254 T1-weighted brain MRI volumes from clinical and population-based cohorts. We evaluate GSMR3D against geometry-specific specialists for isotropic, in-plane, and through-plane super-resolution using reconstruction quality and downstream tasks: brain tumour segmentation, brain-metastasis detection, and brain parcellation. GSMR3D outperforms specialists at isotropic $2\times$ and achieves better LPIPS, DISTS, and FID across all geometries, although specialists retain higher PSNR in anisotropic settings. It more faithfully preserves glioma segmentations across all geometries, achieves the highest lesion sensitivity on an independent brain-metastasis cohort, and the highest brain-parcellation agreement for isotropic and in-plane reconstruction. Reconstruction takes about 6s per volume, adding little overhead relative to acquisition-time savings. GSMR3D offers a flexible approach to MRI scan-time reduction with one model per contrast across geometries while preserving information relevant to downstream tasks.

Title: FlowMaker: From Tasks to Cost-Efficient Generative AI Workflow Deployments

  • Authors: Dimitrios Liakopoulos, Prasoon Sinha, Neeraja J. Yadwadkar
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24861
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.24861
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.24861]] FlowMaker: From Tasks to Cost-Efficient Generative AI Workflow Deployments(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24861)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: Generative AI workflows execute user tasks by composing sequences of LLM calls, where each call performs a sub-task (e.g., summarization or translation), forming a workflow. Executing such workflows requires decisions over workflow structure, model assignments, and deployment configurations, which jointly determine latency, cost, and accuracy under dynamic workloads and resource constraints. Existing systems optimize these decisions in isolation or fix them early, leading to suboptimal cost and goodput. We make two key observations. First, efficient serving requires jointly exploring all combinations of workflows, model choices, and deployment configurations under dynamic cluster state. Second, reuse of deployed LLMs is critical for reducing cost, but must be performed on-the-fly, as reuse depends on current load and resource availability. Together, these make selecting a workflow-variant-a combination of workflow, models, and deployment configuration-a time-critical problem. We present FlowMaker, which exposes a task-level interface and selects workflow-variants on behalf of users. To realize this, we design a constraint-guided load-aware pruning algorithm for fast, on-the-fly decisions. FlowMaker improves goodput by up to 10.14$\times$ and reduces cost by up to 2.77$\times$ compared to state-of-the-art baselines, including Murakkab.

Title: The Missing Compliance Class: LLM Graders That Collapse Jailbreak Success, and a Free Check

  • Authors: Ruqiya Bin Safi
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.CL, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24872
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.24872

  • Keywords: attack
  • Abstract: Jailbreak papers report an attack success rate, the share of responses that deliver the harmful request, and read it as a property of the target model. We hold the target model, the prompts and the responses fixed and change only the grader. Three dense graders of 8B and 9B parameters, from three model families, move compliant responses to the neighbouring class, evasion, instead of scattering them across classes. One of them assigns the compliance label zero times in 900 responses. This class collapse also appears on new harmful requests, on a target model from a third family, and in a held-out 7B grader from a fourth family. Per-class label counts expose it at no cost and without human labels. The collapse decides how the rate behaves under a change of grader. On two corpora of 900 responses and eight graders, the compliance rate differs by a factor of 22 to 25, while the non-refusal rate differs by 18 to 23 percentage points. Without the three collapsing graders, compliance differs by a factor of 1.7 to 2.8 on all four corpora, and non-refusal still differs by 10 to 22 points. These results do not change when every grader is called once. We propose a reporting minimum that keeps the compliance rate and adds its per-class label counts and the named grader. Beside it goes the non-refusal rate, whose disagreement across graders is additive.

  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24897
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.24897
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.24897]] Distribution-Based Regularization for Reward Fine-Tuning Diffusion and Flow Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24897)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Reward fine-tuning is an effective way to adapt pretrained diffusion and flow-matching models to downstream objectives, but aggressive optimization often leads to mode collapse and distributional drift. Existing methods attempt to control this drift with reverse-KL regularization, implemented as an $\ell_2$ penalty on the velocity field; however, this functions by indirectly constraining sampling dynamics rather than directly acting on the generated distribution. To address this, we propose DFR (Distributional Functional Rewards), which augments the task reward with a distribution-dependent, per-sample reward that acts explicitly on the generated distribution. Formally, we generalize the KL-regularized objective from expected rewards, which are linear in the model's distribution, to convex functionals of it. The first variation of this objective defines an effective, distribution-dependent reward that directly penalizes deviation from the pretrained distribution. This formulation yields additive per-sample rewards for the Fréchet distance (FD), maximum mean discrepancy (MMD), and DreamSim diversity, recovering several recently proposed rewards as special cases. Across various text-to-image fine-tuning tasks, DFR matches the peak reward of KL-tuned baselines while increasing diversity by up to 29% at equal or lower drift.

  • Keywords: defense
  • Abstract: Language models behind coding agents are post-trained with outcome rewards, and recent methods concentrate credit on the turns that decide the outcome. We find that defensive actions beyond what a task requires escape both. They begin at silent forks, the turns where every next action leads to the same outcome. Such actions leave the success rate unchanged, and frontier models take the disproportionate branch there in up to 91% of decisive actions under implicit budgets. The most direct fix, a checker in the trajectory reward, reaches these forks only through fully proportionate trajectories, leaving 74% of GRPO groups with identical rewards and behavior unchanged. This motivates a rethink of where learning signals belong, namely where behavior diverges, which a behavioral checker can locate and the outcome cannot. Our method, ForkDPO, contrasts a proportionate and a disproportionate action at the same prefix, at silent forks on both sides of the required behavior. On a test set of unseen repositories, it lowers a 4B model's disproportionate behavior from 70.3% to 58.9%, against 67.7% for DPO at randomly chosen turns, establishing behavioral divergence as a learning signal for how agents act beyond whether they finish. Additionally, optimized programs allow for better downstream performance on four different reasoning and problem-solving tasks.

Two assumptions surround this practice: that leniency or strictness is a property of the judge model, and that a rubric can be improved by asking a model to review it. We test both in a controlled grading task with planted flaws and on two human-labeled datasets, SummEdits and RAGTruth. Holding the judge and the outputs fixed, rubric content moves the pass rate of correct outputs from 2% to 100%, whereas changing the judge model or the rendering of the same checks moves it by a few points. Model self-review then drifts the rubric toward the reviser's own standard: it adds requirements the task never stated and deletes checks that human labels had taught, with three reviser models and on all three datasets. To measure and control this, we show that under all-must-pass aggregation the Shapley value of each check has an exact closed form, which yields per-check catch credit, false-fail blame and unique catches in linear time. Attribution shows that self-review deletes the checks that do unique work and keeps the checks that cause false fails. A change-control gate that combines these quantities with a provenance freeze on label-derived checks holds nearly every drifting revision chain at its starting quality, with identical decisions under three judge models.

Title: A Measurement Perspective on Benchmarking LLM-Generated Text Detectors using Item Response Theory

  • Authors: Yanbin Zhao, Zhipeng Chen, Guangsheng Bao, Guandong Zhang, Yan Li
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24972
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.24972
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.24972]] A Measurement Perspective on Benchmarking LLM-Generated Text Detectors using Item Response Theory(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24972)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Benchmarking large language model (LLM)-generated text detectors commonly relies on aggregate detector-level metrics such as accuracy, F1 score, and AUC. Although useful for summarizing overall performance, these metrics provide limited insight into how benchmark composition shapes detector comparisons or which texts distinguish detectors with different response profiles. We propose a measurement-based benchmarking framework for jointly characterizing benchmark heterogeneity and detector behavior using multidimensional item response theory (IRT). By treating benchmark texts as measurement items and detectors as respondents, the framework estimates text-level discrimination and difficulty-location parameters alongside multidimensional detector abilities. We apply the framework to a benchmark of 32K human-written and machine-generated texts across four domains, evaluated by 14 detectors. Detector ability profiles reveal multidimensional and domain-dependent response patterns that are not fully reflected in aggregate performance rankings. As a practical application, clustering texts in the estimated IRT space yields a compact subset of 2,088 texts (6.5\% of the full 32K benchmark) that largely preserves the comparative detector-performance patterns observed on the full benchmark. Overall, the proposed framework provides a principled and interpretable methodology for benchmarking heterogeneous detection technologies while jointly characterizing the measurement properties of the texts used for their evaluation.

  • Authors: Josep Marques, Hyunyoung A. Cho, Youngeun Kim, Youjia Zhang, Aecheon Jung, Kyuhong Shim, Sungeun Hong

  • Abstract: Recursive Transformers depend not only on how many parameters are shared, but also on how shared parameters are organized across depth. We argue that sharing granularity is an important factor in recursive parameter sharing. This study proposes Block Recursive Transformers (BRT), which partition layers into contiguous blocks and recursively reuse each block before moving to the next. Across decoder-only models up to 1.7B parameters, with additional encoder-decoder and encoder-only studies, BRT improves validation negative log-likelihood and downstream accuracy over static recursive sharing baselines. Title: SoK: PIAtlas: Decomposing the Unbounded Prompt Injection Attack Space

  • Authors: Stavros Damianakis, Konstantinos Psounis
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.CL, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24999
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.24999
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.24999]] SoK: PIAtlas: Decomposing the Unbounded Prompt Injection Attack Space(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24999)
  • Keywords: security, defense, attack
  • Abstract: Prompt injection is the dominant security risk for large-language-model (LLM) applications and agents, yet the field still lacks a coherent map of how these attacks are constructed. More than twenty public datasets describe prompt injection using incompatible vocabularies, often organized around delivery vector, attacker goal, or generation method. In this Systematization of Knowledge, we present PIAtlas, a taxonomy organized instead around how the injected instruction is constructed to subvert the underlying model. Its six classes, one per subversion mechanism, form the roots of a hierarchy of techniques and sub-techniques that researchers can use to describe attacks consistently and design more systematic evaluations. We derive the taxonomy from how LLMs are trained, prompted, and influenced by human-like framing, and compare it with historical attack data. To ground this comparison, we release PICorpus, to our knowledge the largest license-clean collection of public prompt-injection datasets, and map thousands of its records to PIAtlas under a human-verified protocol. The mapping shows that attacks usually combine several techniques drawn from only a few mechanisms. It also reveals gaps that existing labels obscure: widely used agent benchmarks test only part of the attack space, and adaptive attackers favor mechanisms that curated benchmarks underrepresent. PIAtlas thus provides a common foundation for understanding this attack space, improving benchmark coverage, and guiding red teaming and defenses.

  • Abstract: Looped large language models trade parameter storage for recurrent computation, but most are trained from scratch. In practice, pretraining with looped depth is expensive, making post-training upcycling of a pretrained non-looped LLM an attractive alternative. We introduce \textbf{UpLoop}, a post-training recipe based on \emph{layer-wise} recurrence. Instead of looping a whole model or a middle block, UpLoop loops each selected layer locally, preserving the pretrained depth hierarchy and avoiding feature-space mismatch. Title: ReTrace: Visual Backtracking for Efficient Multimodal Large Language Models

  • Authors: Guangzong Si, Dong Wang, Yifan Yu, Miao Liu, Panwang Pan, Wentao Zhu
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25005
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.25005
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.25005]] ReTrace: Visual Backtracking for Efficient Multimodal Large Language Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25005)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) encode images into dense visual token sequences, creating substantial attention and KV-cache overhead. Existing visual token compression methods reduce this cost by pruning low-saliency tokens in shallow layers, but this practice couples temporary computational deactivation with irreversible information deletion. We show that this coupling breaks an intrinsic inference pattern in MLLMs. Through layer-wise attention analysis, we identify \textbf{Visual Backtracking}: visual pivots remain spatially stable, yet in middle layers they actively retrieve context from tokens that appeared unimportant earlier. Thus, early low-saliency tokens can be inactive for computation while still being useful for later reasoning. Motivated by this finding, we propose \textbf{ReTrace}, a backtracking-aware compression framework that introduces a dormant token state. ReTrace caches non-pivot tokens outside expensive layer-wise computation, restores them during the backtracking window, and prunes only after their information has been absorbed. Experiments on Qwen2.5-VL and InternVL3 demonstrate nearly 90\% reductions in FLOPs and KV cache, a 2.83$\times$ prefilling speedup, and strong performance retention across complex vision-language benchmarks.

Title: Multifidelity Monte Carlo Estimator for High-Dimensional Quantities of Interest

  • Authors: Zhibo Dai, Hamdi A. Tchelepi, Daniel M. Tartakovsky
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25016
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.25016

  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Multifidelity Monte Carlo (MFMC) reduces the cost of estimating the statistics of quantities of interest (QoIs) computed via expensive high-fidelity model by using cheaper low-fidelity models as control variates. We extend MFMC to vector-valued QoIs by replacing the scalar control-variate coefficients with matrices and minimizing a weighted mean-squared error. The optimal full-matrix coefficients are available in closed form, and their variance reduction is summarized by an effective correlation that takes the place of the squared Pearson correlation in the closed-form MFMC design. Restricting the coefficients to block-diagonal, diagonal, and scalar classes gives a nested hierarchy of estimators with increasing variance reduction and increasing pilot-data demand, with closed-form coefficients for compatible weightings. For a scalar QoI obtained by linearly projecting the state, correcting the full state before projecting is never worse at the population level. Experiments on a Gaussian problem, a reduced-order model of an advection--diffusion problem, and a coarse-grid two-phase flow problem show that the best class depends on the pilot size relative to the number of covariance quantities it must estimate, and that reusing the pilot samples lowers the mean-squared error.

Title: Synergy-Aware Residual Flow Matching for Single-Cell Perturbation Prediction

  • Authors: Jingrui Wu, Wangyun Dan
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI

To preserve population-level response structure, we align both endpoint distributions and biological residual distributions with MMD-based regularization. We evaluate SynGeneFlow on genetic and drug perturbation benchmarks under observed-single, held-out, and cross-modality settings. The results demonstrate that residualizing flow matching around additive perturbation effects provides a biologically grounded and robust framework for single-cell perturbation response prediction.

Title: The Optimal Sample Complexity of Pointwise Replicable Agnostic Learning

  • Authors: Guangjian Zhang
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25062
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.25062
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.25062]] The Optimal Sample Complexity of Pointwise Replicable Agnostic Learning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25062)
  • Keywords: protect
  • Abstract: Pointwise replicability asks that two independently trained predictors, using the same random seed, agree at each fixed query with high probability. We determine its optimal agnostic sample complexity on finite and countable domains. For every nonempty binary class of finite VC dimension $d\ge1$, the required number of independent examples is $\Theta\!\left(\frac{d}{\rho^2\alpha^2}+\frac{\log(1/\beta)}{\alpha^2}\right)$, uniformly over independent accuracy, failure, and disagreement parameters $\alpha,\beta,\rho\in(0,1/10)$. The learner returns one binary function, may be improper, and uses unrestricted shared randomness. Its sample budget is fixed before observing data, and the replicability guarantee includes queries of zero marginal mass. The main construction combines an ensemble of empirical-risk minimizers with a smooth, signal-dependent risk decomposition. A single shared phase selects the final binary predictor. Record times certify its weak-signal risk, while a uniform bound protects the strong-signal risk at that same selected time. A phase-comparison theorem controls the actual shared-seed execution under simultaneous changes in the marginals and risk coefficients. A rare repair step then makes the confidence cost additive. Together with matching lower bounds, this removes the remaining logarithmic overhead and parameter restrictions in the general countable-domain sample-complexity problem. This extended version also retains the earlier explicit constructions and their proofs, including proper learning for VC-one classes, the full finite-atom class, a faster realizable bound, and counterexamples that separate restrictions of particular algorithms from restrictions of the learning problem.

  • Keywords: robust, transformer
  • Abstract: Understanding when during a video a model makes its critical observations is essential for interpreting video-understanding systems. We introduce Sufficient Frame Subsets: compact sets of frames in which all other frames are masked, yet each subset independently preserves the model's prediction.   • Abstract: Video Reasoning Segmentation (VRS) requires a model to identify an implicitly described target and predict its pixel-level mask trajectory, while resolving the spatial relations and temporal events that determine the answer. We introduce \textbf{Temporal Chain of Video Primitives (TCoVP)}, a spatiotemporally grounded form of Video CoT that organizes query-relevant observations into a chronological reasoning chain. Title: Conformal Prediction-Enabled Sequence-level Policy Optimization for Reasoning Language Models

  • Authors: Hui Yang, Wan Tian, Tao Ren, Zhongyi Li, Yijie Peng
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25092
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.25092
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.25092]] Conformal Prediction-Enabled Sequence-level Policy Optimization for Reasoning Language Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25092)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Reinforcement learning improves the reasoning capability of large language models through verifiable rewards for mathematical and coding tasks. In token-level methods such as GRPO, clipping acts on individual token ratios while the reward and advantage apply to a complete response. Recent sequence-level methods align these units but still use fixed clipping bands, whose tail probabilities change as the score distribution evolves. We propose Conformal Prediction-Enabled Sequence-level Policy Optimization (CSPO), which adaptively calibrates sequence-level clipping bounds using recent training statistics. The resulting band targets a specified marginal tail budget and adapts to policy drift; calibration conditional on response length requires additional assumptions. We bound the probability that a fresh score falls outside the band by the target miscoverage level plus explicit CDF discrepancy and drift terms. Under additional moment conditions, this bound also controls the objective and gradient error relative to the unclipped GSPO power surrogate. Experiments with the Qwen3-1.7B series show improvements over GSPO on difficult mathematical reasoning benchmarks and ALFWorld, with clipping endpoints that adapt during training.

  • Abstract: Healthcare artificial intelligence is shifting from centralized archives toward distributed pipelines that combine wearables, edge processing, cloud analytics, and multi-institutional learning. Title: Deep Fusion Memory: A Chunk-Level Parametric Memory for Large Language Models

  • Authors: Rubin Wei, Siyuan Sun, Hao Sun, Yuliang Liu, Xiang Shi, Jiarui Wang, Junming Zhang, Zhouhan Lin
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25100
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.25100
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.25100]] Deep Fusion Memory: A Chunk-Level Parametric Memory for Large Language Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25100)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models need effective ways to acquire and use external knowledge beyond what they retain from pretraining. Continued pretraining risks catastrophic forgetting, while retrieval at inference time can add substantial overhead. External parametric memory avoids online retrieval, but constructing its training data requires extensive offline search, and output-level interpolation provides only shallow fusion. We introduce Deep Fusion Memory (DFM), a parametric memory architecture that reduces this construction workload and incorporates learned memory into intermediate hidden states. A causal memory predictor learns to generate memory from the preceding context, using retrieved chunk embeddings from the corpus as supervision. The predicted memory is shared across tokens within each chunk and fused into their hidden states through gated cross-attention. Memory prediction and fusion are trained end to end with the backbone frozen, and inference requires no explicit retrieval. Compared with existing parametric memory approaches, which often construct token-level memory targets, DFM requires $32\times$ fewer retrieval queries and $2048\times$ fewer retrieved items to construct its training data. Across six QA tasks, DFM performs better over the strongest baseline by 12.71% on Mistral-7B-v0.3 and 11.19% on Qwen3-8B-Base. Beyond QA, domain adaptation yields average gains of 6.51 and 4.38 points over Qwen3-4B-Base and Qwen3-8B-Base, respectively.

Title: Generation-Heavy Hybrid Machine Translation

  • Authors: Nizar Yahya Habash
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25107
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.25107
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.25107]] Generation-Heavy Hybrid Machine Translation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25107)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: The state of the art techniques in Machine Translation (MT) require large amounts of symmetric resources from source and target languages. This is true regardless of whether the approach is Transfer or Interlingua, Symbolic or Statistical. Symmetry within these approaches is necessary to ensure quality, robustness and retargetability. In reality, such symmetry, whether it is in terms of structural transfer lexicons, interlingual dictionaries or parallel corpora, is a major bottleneck in developing any MT system. This dissertation presents an approach to MT that addresses the lack of symmetry by exploiting symbolic and statistical target language resources in source-poor/target-rich language pairs. This approach is called Generation-Heavy Hybrid Machine Translation (GHMT). Expected source language resources include a syntactic parser and a simple one-to-many translation dictionary. No transfer rules or complex interlingual representations are used. Rich target language symbolic resources are used to overgenerate multiple structural variations from a target-glossed syntactic dependency representation of source language sentences. Statistical target-language resource are then used to select amongst the overgenerated translations. The source-target asymmetry of systems developed in this approach makes them more easily retargetable to new source languages.

Title: How Much Feedback Is Needed to Break Template Protection? The Geometric Vulnerability of Biometric Authentication

  • Authors: Eliron Rahimi, Margarita Osadchy, Orr Dunkelman
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25118
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.25118
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.25118]] How Much Feedback Is Needed to Break Template Protection? The Geometric Vulnerability of Biometric Authentication(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25118)
  • Keywords: security, privacy, protect, attack, biometric, diffusion
  • Abstract: Biometric authentication systems increasingly use privacy-preserving mechanisms to protect sensitive templates. However, the authentication interface itself may still leak information that enables reconstruction attacks. Prior attacks exploit released similarity scores, whereas more advanced systems reveal only the binary accept/reject decision. This binary feedback provides substantially less information, making template reconstruction far more challenging. In this work, we introduce query-efficient reconstruction attacks under extremely limited feedback, addressing two key limitations of prior attacks. First, we introduce the first attack that reconstructs protected biometric templates using only authentication success/failure feedback by exploiting the geometric constraints of biometric authentication. Second, we introduce distribution-aware query-efficient reconstruction methods for score-releasing systems, achieving a more realistic threat model by reducing the number of required queries by a factor of $5\times$ compared to recent prior score-based attacks. Finally, we combine these two insights by incorporating a pretrained facial diffusion prior into our geometric binary attack, reducing the number of required queries by a factor of $5\times$. Our findings reveal a fundamental security limitation of biometric authentication: independently of the template protection mechanism, the authentication process itself exposes geometric information that can enable perfect template reconstruction up to numerical precision. Moreover, the distribution of biometric templates can be leveraged to make significantly more effective reconstruction attacks.

Title: Memory as Belief: Bayesian Evolution of Memory for Self-Evolving LLM Agents

  • Authors: Yiming Xu, Qian Yang, Zihan Chen, Yuefei Wu, Wenhan Zeng, Bin Shi, Bo Dong
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25119
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.25119

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly rely on external memory to accumulate past experiences for continual adaptation. However, effectively reusing memory requires accurately assessing its value, as retrieved experiences may become unreliable when transferred across task contexts. Despite its importance, memory valuation remains largely underexplored, with existing methods relying on historical retrieval success rates that overlook the context dependency of memory usefulness and the varying reliability of estimates under different amounts of evidence. Inspired by the Bayesian brain hypothesis, we propose BEAM (Bayesian-style Evolutionary Agentic Memory), a framework that reformulates memory value evaluation as a parameterized cognitive inference process, where memory value is represented as a context-conditioned dynamic belief rather than a passive historical statistic. Specifically, BEAM infers memory value beliefs from current task contexts and continuously calibrates them using execution feedback as empirical evidence, transforming memory from passive storage into an adaptive cognitive mechanism for continual self-evolution.   • Abstract: Offline sepsis treatment aims to learn treatment policies from retrospective ICU trajectories. During inference, ClinSDT performs closed-loop treatment rollout by refreshing semantic inputs from the currently available patient state.   • Abstract: Skill-augmented agentic RL has emerged as an effective approach to improving LLM agents on long-horizon tasks. However, existing skill library systems fail to identify each skill's true contribution due to free-riding among co-retrieved skills, difficulty confounding across tasks, and the absence of skill-specific counterfactual evidence, which prevents reliable skill lifecycle management. We propose TrackRL, an agentic RL framework where skill tracking drives skill library full lifecycle management. Specifically, within each GRPO group, we turn grouped sampling into online randomized controlled experiments. Independent random injection and within-group contrasts on the same task isolate each skill's true contribution, and reliability-weighted aggregation accumulates evidence across groups. As evidence accumulates, we continuously track skill utility, which measures the skill's causal contribution as external scaffolding, and model mastery, which reflects how far the corresponding capability has been internalized. Along this line, we formulate skill lifecycle management as an evidence-gated state-transition process that governs each skill from generation and admission through protected incubation to retention, retirement, or removal, while a soft capacity bound controls library growth. Experiments show that TrackRL outperforms recent skill-augmented RL baselines in both library-assisted and skill-free evaluation, with further analyses revealing how skill value shifts during policy learning and how evidence-driven state transitions shape skill library evolution. Our code is available at this https URL.

  • Abstract: Cryptographic API misuse in large language model-generated code can compromise software security. To investigate this, we construct 48,000 matched program pairs across four programming languages and ten cryptographic API misuse categories. Each pair changes a specified API decision while preserving the programming task. Subtracting the misuse program's internal representation from its secure counterpart's gives a repair vector.   • Abstract: A retailer makes different decisions at different grains of the product, store and time hierarchy: store orders for an item in a store, supplier funding for an item across stores, category performance by store, and the financial plan for the chain. Which forecasting model to use depends on the grain. This paper compares models at each grain on four public data sets (M5, six Dominick's categories, Breakfast at the Frat, and Corporación Favorita, the last kept out of all development), over rolling forecast origins with paired tests and model confidence sets. It covers price and promotion through the plan inputs these data record, not product, assortment or place. The models are a world model that carries each item's state forward under the price and promotion plan, the same network without that state, LightGBM, the Temporal Fusion Transformer, TimesFM, Chronos-Bolt, Timestral, seasonal naive, and an equal-weight average of the world model, LightGBM and TimesFM given the plan. The answer changed with the grain. For an item by store and an item across stores, the average had the lowest error on seven and eight of nine data sets, and the 90% confidence set held a median of 1 and 2 models. For a category by store, a category and the chain total, the lowest-error model differed between data sets and a median of 4 of nine models were not separated by the four to six windows available. For category-by-store planning cost, the average or LightGBM was lowest on seven of eight data sets, and the world model was lowest only on M5; it was behind LightGBM item by store on six of eight. Every trained model used the plan, and on the weekly promotion-driven data pretrained models were less accurate without it. These results hold for these data sets; a forecast solved across all four Ps, or a retailer's own data, may give different results. Every model, test and change that lost is reported.

Title: Concept Representations in Vector Space: Toward an Interdisciplinary Study of Meaning Variation

  • Authors: Simon Josef Durstewitz
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.CY
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25184
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.25184
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.25184]] Concept Representations in Vector Space: Toward an Interdisciplinary Study of Meaning Variation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25184)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: An increasing number of disciplines recognize the explanatory potential of meaning-bearing mental structures through which individuals interpret the world and guide their behavior. Concept representations constitute a core class of these structures, emerging from individuals' experiences with concept referents and encoding and organizing their understanding of them. Recognizing a private level of meaning, where concepts are not objectively defined by rules in the world but constitute subjective structures in individuals' minds, raises a fundamental question: do different individuals actually mean the same thing when they invoke the same concept? As latent structures, concept representations are difficult to access and model empirically. The present study introduces a measurement framework that makes such representations empirically accessible through textual traces elicited under standardized conditions. Using transformer-based sentence embeddings, the framework maps standardized responses elicited for concepts into a high-dimensional shared vector space, opening concept representations to the systematic analysis of interindividual variation. It develops three analytical avenues: (1) comparing representational sharedness across concepts, (2) identifying variables that correlate with representational variation, and (3) examining how focal actors or subpopulations deviate from a reference population. Each avenue is illustrated through an empirical proof-of-concept drawing on a large-scale free-association dataset. Together, the demonstrations show how researchers from across disciplines can move from a research question and data source to analysis and interpretation. An accompanying GitHub repository provides a ready-to-use implementation of the framework for existing and custom datasets.

  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25212
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.25212
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.25212]] Calibration-Free Early Stopping for Chain-of-Thought Reasoning via E-processes(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25212)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Reasoning large language models (LLMs) benefit from test-time scaling, motivating adaptive policies that spend tokens when they help and stop early otherwise. Existing rules halt when a confidence or stability signal crosses a threshold, but such thresholds rarely transfer across models and datasets, and validation-calibrated rules lose their guarantee under shifts in test-time horizon or signal dynamics. We reframe adaptive stopping as sequential evidence accumulation along the reasoning trajectory rather than threshold calibration on held-out data. Our framework runs two e-processes on a shared per-chunk signal: a commit stream tracks the signal's position relative to its slow trend, and an abstain stream tracks its signed velocity, making the two anti-correlated by construction. Across various model--dataset combinations, the framework matches or exceeds calibration-based stopping on the token--accuracy Pareto front using zero calibration data, and the same significance level transfers unchanged across cells where calibration becomes infeasible.

  • Abstract: Recent test-time scaling methods improve model performance by increasing inference-time compute. While effective, this approach leaves the streams uncoordinated, and a discovery made in one rollout cannot influence any other. Multi-Sequence Verifiers jointly score candidate answers after generation, but the reasoning streams remain independent during inference. At the same time, fact retrieval performance for pretrained and finetuned BatchParScale models remains unclear.

Title: PRICE: Privacy-Preserving Image Classification in Mobile Edge-Cloud Computing with Homomorphic Encryption

  • Authors: Duc-Huy Nguyen, Bui Duc Manh, Chi-Hieu Nguyen, Dinh Thai Hoang, Diep N. Nguyen, Van-Dinh Nguyen, Quoc-Viet Pham
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25286
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.25286
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.25286]] PRICE: Privacy-Preserving Image Classification in Mobile Edge-Cloud Computing with Homomorphic Encryption(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25286)
  • Keywords: security, privacy, extraction
  • Abstract: Cloud-assisted image classification enables many mobile AI applications, but transmitting raw images to the cloud raises significant privacy concerns. Homomorphic encryption (HE) allows computation over encrypted data; however, existing end-to-end HE-based inference pipelines incur prohibitive latency and computational overhead, limiting their feasibility for latency-sensitive mobile systems. This paper addresses how to enable efficient encrypted image classification in mobile edge-cloud environments without sacrificing accuracy. We propose PRICE, a three-tier edge-cloud architecture that partitions deep neural network inference between a trusted edge and an untrusted cloud. The edge performs lightweight feature extraction in plaintext, while only compact embeddings are encrypted using the Cheon-Kim-Kim-Song (CKKS) scheme and offloaded to the cloud for encrypted final-layer inference. This design avoids homomorphic evaluation of deep backbone layers, significantly reducing computation depth, ciphertext size, and end-to-end latency. To further improve efficiency, we develop RowHyDiag, a row-packed hybrid diagonal encoding with hoisted-rotation-aware encrypted matrix-vector computation, which minimizes rotation operations and improves slot utilization. Extensive experiments on real devices across five datasets demonstrate that, under the evaluated 128-bit-security configurations, RowHyDiag achieves up to 31.8$\times$ faster cloud inference, 85.3$\times$ fewer rotations, and 83.0$\times$ lower setup payload than the conventional Halevi-Shoup diagonal method. RowHyDiag also outperforms the PEGASUS linear-transformation baseline, providing 1.1-4.5$\times$ faster cloud inference and 1.2-3.4$\times$ lower setup payload. The results show that PRICE enables deployment-ready, low-latency encrypted inference for mobile edge-cloud systems under a trusted-edge threat model.

Title: The Explainability Illusion: Must ViTs Exhibit Patch-Decoding Interpretability?

  • Authors: Ronen Schaffer, Hila Manor, Tomer Michaeli
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25289
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.25289
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.25289]] The Explainability Illusion: Must ViTs Exhibit Patch-Decoding Interpretability?(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25289)
  • Keywords: interpretability, explainability, transformer
  • Abstract: Transformers are widely assumed to exhibit token-decoding explainability. In particular, in vision transformer (ViT) classifiers, decoding individual patch tokens has become a common diagnostic tool due to its tendency to yield human-interpretable outputs. In light of the increasing popularity of this approach, an important question is whether token-decoding explainability is an inherent property of transformers that should be expected to hold across architectures, scales, and training objectives, or merely an artifact of some implicit bias. In this paper we systematically study token-decoding explainability across diverse ViT settings, spanning architectures, scales, and training objectives, using both controlled experiments and real-world models. We find that while some models indeed exhibit aligned and interpretable token decodings, others exhibit either inverted explainability maps or a complete lack of correlation with the image semantics. As we show, these tendencies toward different explainability behaviors are not intrinsic to the architecture and can vary arbitrarily with changes in the optimization landscape. We provide theoretical support for these observations by demonstrating a setting where a single fixed toy transformer architecture can provably implement the optimal classification rule in multiple ways, which differ in their patch-decoding explainability patterns. Our results show that token-decoding explainability is not an inherent property of trained transformers, but rather an outcome contingent on the problem setting and thus should not be a-priori assumed to hold. Code can be found in this https URL .

Title: MotionShift: Environment-Compatible Motion Transfer via Planner-Evaluator Synergistic Adaptation

  • Authors: Yanchen Liu, Junyao Gao, Yanan Sun, Wensheng Chen, Guangming Lu, Wenjie Pei
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25290
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.25290
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.25290]] MotionShift: Environment-Compatible Motion Transfer via Planner-Evaluator Synergistic Adaptation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25290)
  • Keywords: generative, large language model
  • Abstract: Existing motion transfer methods excel at exact, rigid motion replication from reference videos to target scenes, whereas they often fail to account for the compatibility between the transferred motion and the target scene environment in accordance with physical laws. In this work, we propose MotionShift, a novel motion transfer framework that achieves high-fidelity motion transfer while ensuring physical compatibility with the target scene. In particular, we design a planner--evaluator iterative motion adaptation mechanism, in which a Large Language Model (LLM) acts as the planner to generate motion trajectories by leveraging its strong semantic reasoning capabilities, while a Vision-Language Model (VLM) serves as the evaluator to rectify the motion based on its superior visual perception and scene understanding. Through this planner--evaluator synergistic reasoning process, the motion is adapted to the target scene in a training-free manner. Extensive experiments demonstrate that MotionShift achieves high-fidelity and physically compatible motion transfer across diverse and complex scenes. Moreover, we show that MotionShift can be readily integrated into existing trajectory-conditioned I2V models, endowing them with realistic motion transfer capabilities.

  • Abstract: Emerging Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures (JEPAs) are moving beyond representation learning towards latent world models that support prediction, planning, and control. Title: Learning When to Abort in Transformer Planning: A Mechanistic Study

  • Authors: Shi Feng, Qimin Zhong, Siwei Wang, Yifei Shen, Shang-Hua Teng, Hao Liao, Wei Chen
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25300
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.25300
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.25300]] Learning When to Abort in Transformer Planning: A Mechanistic Study(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25300)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Planning does not by itself imply the ability to recognise errors, but recognising errors can improve planning capacity. In this paper, we study this phenomenon in Transformer planning. To analyze the inner mechanism, we follow the approach of Wang et al. (2024) and abstract planning as path-finding in graphs. In this setting, we train the model to detect the error of invalid transitions, i.e., after the model generates a next node that is not adjacent with the current node, the model should then generate a special abort token x. For expressiveness, we construct a Transformer with feed-forward width $O(|V|)$ for any fixed graph with node set $V$. It can detect all possible invalid transitions, and select a valid next node if the prefix is valid. We then propose a worst-case lower bound on feed-forward width, which makes the detector's linear width near-optimal up to logarithmic factors. In trained models, we demonstrate that invalid-transition detection increases previous-node attention, and most of the valid--invalid separation locates in the feed-forward network's output. This accords with our theoretical insights. Finally, we use this error detection to improve the model's performance: at inference, generating the abort token x will directly cause an external procedure to discard the attempt and restart from the original prompt. Experiments show that error detection combined with retry improves model's planning reliability on graphs, Countdown, and mazes.

Title: Equivalent Non-linear Shift Register Transformations and Espresso

  • Abstract: The stream cipher Espresso was designed by E. Dubrova and M. Hell as a hardware-efficient candidate for 5G security. However, the security of Espresso with regard to several standard cryptanalytic attacks are estimated using an alternate NFSR, which has only two feedback taps. Next, in the context of Espresso equivalence, we analyze and unify the transformation framework of Dubrova and the one proposed by Yao and Parampalli. Based on our analysis, we have succeeded in transforming the LFSR in an Espresso-equivalent design reported by Yao and Parampalli to the Espresso alternate NFSR. Further, we have obtained a corresponding non-linear output function which achieves output-equivalence with the LFSR design, and hence, with Espresso.

Title: Operator-Mismatch Contrastive Learning for World-Relative Trajectory Coherence

  • Authors: One Octadion, Firmansyah Sundana, Novanto Yudistira
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25360
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.25360
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.25360]] Operator-Mismatch Contrastive Learning for World-Relative Trajectory Coherence(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25360)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: World models are increasingly judged by how well they rank plausible futures, yet plausibility alone is the wrong target. The same scene can evolve under many latent rules, and a future that is smooth and physically valid under one rule can be incoherent under the rule actually in force. We formalize the resulting failure, operator-blindness, and show that it is systematically hidden by standard evaluation: a scorer can attain near-perfect generic ranking while separating current-world from other-world futures no better than chance. We introduce Operator-Mismatch Contrastive (OMC) learning, whose semantic hard negatives are valid-but-wrong-operator trajectories, and a matching protocol that measures world-relative coherence directly. On a controlled synthetic benchmark with in- and out-of-distribution operator parameters, across two trajectory-scoring backbones, and on feature-level external validation with CoPhy ballsCF, OMC raises operator-sensitive scoring far above a chance baseline that nonetheless achieves 0.99 generic top-1. We prove that the wrong-operator negative is necessary both for the protocol to detect the failure and for the objective to fix it, and position OMC as an auxiliary coherence signal (a trajectory-coherence scorer, not a generative model, planner, or control agent) that can be attached to existing world-model representations.

Title: SEAL: Towards Persistent Tool Poisoning Attack against LLM Agents via Prefix-Eliciting Trigger
