  • Keywords: security, defense, attack
  • Abstract: Tool poisoning has emerged as a critical security threat to LLM agents, where adversaries embed malicious instructions in tool descriptions or external resources that enter the agent's context, allowing adversaries to manipulate agent's tool selection process. However, existing tool poisoning attacks primarily rely on an idealized assumption that the attacks task effect within a single interaction round. We show that their effectiveness degrades sharply in realistic multi-round agentic settings. We further reveal that this degradation stems from the fact that agents do not consistently surface attack-relevant signals in their reasoning as the planning context evolves, thereby weakening the attack's influence across rounds. To investigate whether tool poisoning remains a practical threat in such realistic settings, we propose SEAL, a two-stage method for persistent tool poisoning. Specifically, SEAL first performs Prefix-Eliciting Trigger Optimization (PETO) to optimize context-specific triggers that elicit attack-relevant reasoning prefixes, improving effectiveness within individual contexts. It then applies Local-to-Universal Trigger Aggregation (L2U) to combine these local triggers into a single universal trigger, improving generalization across different contexts and execution rounds. Extensive experiments across seven representative multi-round scenarios and different models demonstrate the attack effectiveness of SEAL, its transferability across target models, and its resistance to potential defenses.

Title: Paloma: Phase-Conditioned Residual Modulation for Time Series Forecasting

  • Authors: Jingru Fei, Wei Fan, Kun Yi, Qi Zhang, Zhendong Niu
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25392
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.25392

  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Explicitly modeling periodicity has proven to be an efficient approach for time series forecasting, where the residual, obtained after removing the periodic component from the time series, is typically treated as unstructured noise. However, in this work, we show that such residuals still exhibit structured, \textit{phase-dependent} deviations, as supported by both empirical observations and theoretical analysis. This suggests that residuals should not be treated merely as unstructured noise, but instead as \textit{phase-conditioned signals} whose variations arise in both direction and magnitude. Motivated by this perspective and inspired by phase and amplitude modulation in classical signal processing, we propose \textit{Paloma}, a simple yet effective framework for modeling residual deviations. Specifically, Paloma comprises two complementary modules: \textit{a Phase Rotation module}, which performs phase-conditioned rotations to capture directional changes in the feature space, and \textit{an Amplitude Scaling module}, which applies phase-conditioned affine transformations to model magnitude variations. We further abstract the two modules into a general-purpose plugin, termed the \textit{Paloma technique}, which can be readily integrated into existing forecasting architectures to modulate input sequences in a phase-aware manner, enabling backbone models to capture phase-dependent variations in both direction and magnitude. Extensive experiments on twelve datasets demonstrate that Paloma achieves state-of-the-art and robust forecasting performance. Additional results show that the Paloma technique consistently improves a wide range of baseline models, validating its effectiveness as a general-purpose component. Our Code is available at \url{this https URL}.

We introduce code-switched task synthesis for cross-lingual RLVR, a data-centric approach that derives target-language and code-switched RLVR task variants from otherwise unverifiable English domain text. Using fill-in-the-middle multiple-choice QA as a seed task format, we synthesize automatically verifiable tasks from domain passages and reshape them into cross-lingual variants. These variants preserve the same evidence-answer relation but change how English and target-language evidence are presented during RLVR. On Korean medical benchmarks and cybersecurity benchmarks across four target languages, code-switched training outperforms matched monolingual and bilingual-mixture baselines on average target-language performance while maintaining comparable English performance. Additional analyses provide evidence of better target-language input understanding and stronger cross-lingual alignment in both model outputs and internal representations. These results suggest that code-switched training helps target-language inputs access domain reasoning learned more strongly in English.

Title: MT Forcing: Towards Stable and Consistent Multi-Turn Image Editing with Autoregressive Diffusion

  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25404
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.25404
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.25404]] MT Forcing: Towards Stable and Consistent Multi-Turn Image Editing with Autoregressive Diffusion(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25404)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Multi-turn image editing aims to modify an image according to a sequence of instructions, enabling more effective interactive visual content creation for users. However, existing methods often exhibit noticeable drift after only a few editing turns, manifesting in two aspects: 1) unstable visual quality, with accumulating artifacts and degraded details; and 2) inconsistent image content, with unintended changes to preserved regions and prior edits. To address these challenges, we propose MT Forcing, a novel training paradigm for multi-turn image editing with unified multimodal models (UMMs) based on autoregressive diffusion. Specifically, we propose Single-Turn Distribution Matching Distillation (Single-Turn DMD), which supervises student self-rollouts with a teacher that applies cumulative edits to the source image in a single turn, providing visual-quality guidance without recursive error accumulation. Additionally, we introduce Teacher-Forcing Guidance, which supervises history-dependent transitions on reference trajectories during warmup and as a regularizer during distillation, encouraging cross-turn content consistency beyond what per-turn distribution matching alone can ensure. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our approach significantly improves the multi-turn image editing capabilities of UMMs, yielding more stable visual quality and stronger cross-turn consistency.

Title: From Label Priors to Task Evidence: Efficient Long-Video Frame Selection via Bayesian GRPO

  • Authors: Shuochen Chang, Bingjie Gao, Qingyang Liu, Qianli Ma, Yibo Miao, Haonan Zhao, Zhaohe Liao, Xiaofeng Zhang, Jiangtong Li, Li Niu

  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.25418

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) face significant scalability challenges in long-video understanding due to quadratic attention complexity and context window constraints. Existing retrieval-based approaches typically rely on supervised learning from static keyframe annotations. However, these label priors often fail to align with the actual information required by downstream MLLMs for complex reasoning, creating a misalignment between the training target and inference needs. To bridge this gap, we propose a probabilistic framework that reformulates frame selection as latent-variable inference. We first initialize a lightweight policy using supervised learning to capture general visual saliency. Then, we treat a frozen MLLM as a task environment and use its answer likelihood as dense task evidence to refine the policy via Bayesian Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). Within this formulation, the negative log-likelihood (NLL) over ground-truth answer tokens provides a principled dense reward, while a prior-matching KL term preserves semantic consistency with the elicited label prior. This process transitions the selection principle from static label priors to task evidence. Extensive experiments on Video-MME, MLVU, and LongVideoBench demonstrate that our selector achieves competitive accuracy with MLLM-based scorers while retaining the inference efficiency of lightweight methods. The selector is trained once with a frozen MLLM environment and transfers directly to multiple downstream MLLMs without retraining. Code is available at \url{this http URL}.

Title: Adaptive Cyber-Defense for Evolving Attacker Capabilities using Reinforcement Learning and Behavior Trees

  • Authors: Nicholas Potteiger, Ankita Samaddar, Xenofon Koutsoukos

  • Abstract: Autonomous cyber-defense agents trained using reinforcement learning (RL) are increasingly adopted for defending enterprise computer networks. Title: Mixture-of-Memories: Low-Overhead Language Modeling with Modular Knowledge Injection

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models store substantial factual knowledge in their parameters, yet this knowledge is difficult to control, update, and reliably elicit at inference time. We propose Mixture-of-Memories (MoM), a memory-augmented language modeling framework that separates routed neural memory from writable factual memory. MoM combines a frozen base LM with two neural memory experts: a latent memory that provides low-latency hidden-state corrections and a lexical memory that provides a token-space knowledge pathway. A lightweight Budget Router sets per-token mixture weights from uncertainty and disagreement features, replacing the fixed interpolation of prior memory modules.   • Abstract: The Key-Value (KV) cache is the main memory bottleneck of long-context LLM inference. We present MOSAIC, a multi-objective neural architecture search that learns per-layer KV-cache budgets for any eviction method. On both models, it reaches the score of uniform allocation with up to 4x less KV cache (75% smaller). Against EvolKV, MOSAIC explores a ~10^40 x larger search space with 4x fewer evaluations and 18% fewer GPU-hours per budget, and scores higher in 5 of 6 settings. Title: Enhanced Masked Diffusion Unlearning

  • Authors: Tomer Garber, Tom Tirer
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25447
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.25447
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.25447]] Enhanced Masked Diffusion Unlearning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25447)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Masked diffusion language models (MDLMs) generate text by iteratively unmasking tokens with bidirectional context. As these models reach real-world deployment, machine unlearning becomes essential. This process aims to remove the influence of a designated "forget set" without retraining from scratch, so that sensitive, copyrighted, or restricted content can no longer be elicited while preserving retained capabilities. Masked Diffusion Unlearning (MDU) is the first publicly available unlearning method customized for these models: it tunes the model $p_\theta$ to match a frozen prompt-masked anchor $p_{\mathrm{anchor}}$ on the forget set by minimizing the anchor-last divergence $\mathrm{KL}(p_\theta \Vert p_{\mathrm{anchor}})$. We identify two failure modes of MDU. First, random answer masking can leave key forget tokens visible to the anchor even when the question is fully masked. Second, at a memorized (near-one-hot) prediction, the anchor-last logit gradient vanishes while the anchor-first divergence $\mathrm{KL}(p_{\mathrm{anchor}} \Vert p_\theta)$ logit gradient does not. As a result, MDU training stalls on the tokens we most need to forget. Backed by theoretical and empirical analyses, we propose Enhanced Masked Diffusion Unlearning (EMDU): anchor-first KL divergence toward a frozen anchor whose entire answer span is masked. On TOFU and RWKU benchmarks using LLaDA-8B and Dream-7B, our EMDU matches or exceeds the forgetting of MDU and adapted AR baselines, while better preserving retain utility.

Title: Quality-Controlled Reply and Summary Data for Post-Training Small Language Models in AI Call-Screening Agents

  • Authors: Jiaqi Gan, Tsang Ng, Ankit Raj, Tommy Duong, Xingyu Shen, Kidus Zewde, Huaiyu Guo, Simiao Ren
  • Subjects: cs.CL

  • Keywords: privacy
  • Abstract: Small language models have improved to the point where a phone can plausibly screen its own calls, answering on an absent owner's behalf without sending call content off the device, a privacy-respecting alternative to cloud call handling. Released small models do neither reliably - they raise unwarranted alarms about ordinary callers and write summaries that drop material conditions or report a caller's claims as fact - and real calls supply no reference replies or summaries to learn from. We construct supervision for both tasks from synthetic calls under an information-boundary constraint: a caller writer works from a private scenario brief, a reply writer sees only the conversation up to the current turn, and a summary writer sees the completed transcript. Separate model-based reviews of the full dialogue and the summary decide which calls enter training, yielding a corpus of 3,395 calls with 15,102 targets (11,707 reply, 3,395 summary). Training a single shared LoRA adapter on both tasks, we evaluate six models (0.6-4B parameters) before and after adaptation on 192 held-out calls. Reply qualification rises from 21-54% to 94-97% and summary qualification from 0-6% to 43-72%; all twelve paired gains (six models x two tasks) have bootstrap intervals above zero, and the summary gains hold in every source family. Because one model both screened the training data and scored the outputs, these rates measure agreement with that evaluator's standard rather than human acceptance, which a planned human study will test. After adaptation, coherence and naturalness failures nearly vanish, leaving grounding and usefulness - faithfully tracking who said what, under which conditions - as the remaining challenge.

Title: Steered Not Stirred: Effective Reward Tilting using Surrogate Reward Mixing

  • Authors: Alec Sargood, Olivia Gallup, Georg Maierhofer, Noam Ghenassia, Paul Duckworth, Krisztina Sinkovics, Avishek Joey Bose
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25502
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.25502
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.25502]] Steered Not Stirred: Effective Reward Tilting using Surrogate Reward Mixing(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25502)
  • Keywords: diffusion, generative
  • Abstract: Continuous-time generative models, from diffusion models, flow matching, and now flow maps, owe their practical value to high-fidelity generation and steerability. In many applications, a ground-truth latent ``oracle'' reward $R^*$ is unavailable or expensive to evaluate, e.g., human judgments or experimental measurements. Although the oracle reward is not directly observable, it can often be approximated through multiple cheaper surrogates that, individually, provide incomplete characterizations but collectively reveal substantial information about the downstream task. In this paper, we present a method for composing such surrogates to steer a pretrained model toward the distribution tilted by an unavailable $R^*$. For preferences, the bound additionally accounts for an irreducible mismatch between the Plackett-Luce population target and the conditional mean. Experiments demonstrate improved oracle-tilt fidelity in language generation and ImageNet alignment, and improved joint pose accuracy and validity in protein-ligand cofolding, without inference-time oracle queries.

Title: Dynamics-Aligned Representation Learning under Induced Partial Observability

  • Authors: SM Mazharul Islam, Manfred Huber
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25511
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.25511
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.25511]] Dynamics-Aligned Representation Learning under Induced Partial Observability(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25511)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Model-based reinforcement learning (MBRL) has enabled sample-efficient learning of continuous control policies from high-dimensional observations through learned latent dynamics models. These models are typically trained with observation reconstruction objectives, which provide strong supervision but can bias representations toward visual fidelity leading to an undertrained prior and unreliable long-horizon predictions. We propose a stochastic gating mechanism that induces controlled partial observability during training by sampling a spatial binary mask from a Beta--Bernoulli random field and applying it to the raw observation. The gate probability is resampled at each time step, exposing the encoder to conditions ranging from near-total occlusion to near-full visibility. Integration of our method requires minimal architectural changes from existing dynamics models: the gated observation and the binary mask are channel-wise concatenated and passed to the encoder as a single augmented input, with no modifications to the broader training pipeline. We evaluate the proposed approach on a set of pixel-based continuous control tasks selected from the DeepMind Control Suite. Using model predictive control for evaluation, which relies solely on open-loop model rollouts, we show that stochastic gating improves planning performance and data efficiency across tasks. Further analysis demonstrates that performance is robust to the spatial scale of the mask, and a balanced observability during training outperforms configurations biased toward either sparser or denser observations.

Title: ATEST: Adaptive Resource-Aware Test-Time Scaling for LLM Inference

  • Authors: Mobina Kashaniyan, Ali Jannesari
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.DC, cs.PF
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25519
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.25519
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.25519]] ATEST: Adaptive Resource-Aware Test-Time Scaling for LLM Inference(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25519)
  • Keywords: robust, large language model
  • Abstract: Test-time scaling (TTS) improves large language model (LLM) reasoning by sampling multiple answers, but fixed sampling budgets ignore both query difficulty and the changing energy cost of inference under GPU contention. We measure up to a 4.1-fold increase in energy for the same four-sample query as GPU load rises. We present ATEST, a resource-aware TTS framework that jointly considers the expected value of additional samples and their current GPU cost. ATEST-Online, its default mode, requires no offline calibration: it incrementally generates samples, estimates the stability of the plurality answer, monitors live GPU conditions, and learns step energy cost online to decide when to stop. ATEST-Calibrated instead selects a per-query budget using offline accuracy and cost models, favoring deployments with stable loads. On GSM8K at high load, it achieves 60.0% accuracy with no deferrals, versus 44.0% accuracy and 42.4% deferrals for ATEST-Calibrated. Ablations show that online cost learning drives adaptation to load. Including offline profiling, ATEST-Online uses 42 times less GPU time and 48 times less energy than ATEST-Calibrated for the evaluated workload. Online control trades higher tail latency and lower throughput on short tasks for robustness under varying load; offline calibration remains competitive under stable conditions.

Title: Distributionally-Robust Policy Learning with Observational Data

  • Authors: Debmalya Mandal
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25591
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.25591
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.25591]] Distributionally-Robust Policy Learning with Observational Data(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25591)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Policy learning from observational data is fundamental to applications ranging from personalized medicine to welfare-program targeting, but standard approaches optimize empirical performance and may fail when the deployment distribution differs from the training distribution. We study \emph{distributionally-robust policy learning}: given observational data, we seek a policy $\pi \in \Pi$ that maximizes the worst-case policy value over an $f$-divergence ball of radius $\rho$ around the data-generating distribution. We propose an estimator built on cross-fitted doubly-robust scores for the conditional treatment effect, paired with a DRO solver that handles \texttt{KL}, $\chi^2$, and \texttt{CVaR} ambiguity sets. We establish two convergence guarantees. First, the estimated policy attains the standard $\widetilde{O}(n^{-1/2})$ rate on its DRO regret, and inherits the doubly-robust property -- the rate continues to hold whenever either the outcome model or the propensity model is consistently estimated, even at slow nonparametric rates. We then propose a sample-splitting bias-correction algorithm derived from the Lagrangian dual, and show that under a Tsybakov-style margin condition on the treatment-effect, the estimator attains a fast rate of $\widetilde{O}(n^{-(1+\alpha)/(2+\alpha)})$ , interpolating between $\widetilde{O}(1/\sqrt{n})$ and $\widetilde{O}(1/n)$ as the margin parameter $\alpha$ ranges over $[0,\infty)$. To our knowledge, this is the first work to incorporate a margin condition into the analysis of distributionally-robust policy learning, and the first to obtain fast rates in this setting. Empirically, on synthetic and semi-synthetic experiments calibrated to a welfare-to-work program, distributional robustness yields meaningful improvements over empirical welfare maximization when training and test distributions differ.

However, event-based spacecraft pose estimation (EBSPE) suffers when the relative motion between the chaser and target is slow, since the event rate drops significantly, leading to data with low signal-to-noise ratio. Unfortunately, for safety reasons, real RPO trajectories often involve slow relative motions (e.g., down to 1 mm/s), and operating under low illumination exacerbates the problem. Addressing slow relative motions is thus crucial to raise the practicality of EBSPE. To this end, we propose using recurrent vision transformers (RVT) for EBSPE. The key idea is to exploit recurrent memory to capture longer-term information across sparse event streams, and to learn the temporal attention span from full trajectories rather than fixing the time window. We developed an RVT-based heatmap regressor to produce 2D-3D correspondences for pose estimation, which was validated on real event data collected under slow relative motions representative of RPO trajectories. Results indicate the superior pose estimation accuracy and stability of our RVT-based method, relative to previous per-event-frame-based or fixed-time-window EBSPE methods.

Title: Clean Forcing: Drift-Resistant Autoregressive Video Diffusion with a Frozen Base

  • Authors: Wenqing Wang, Joonghyuk Shin, Jonathan Tremblay, Chan Hee Song, Yun Fu

  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25634

  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Autoregressive (AR) video diffusion models generate streaming video of arbitrary length by conditioning each new chunk of frames on those already generated. However, they suffer from exposure bias, where a model trained on clean context must continue from its own imperfect outputs, and errors compound until quality collapses within seconds (drift). Existing methods either require cluster-scale base retraining, apply training-free inference corrections with inconsistent results, or train frozen-base correctors for sampling and cache errors. We instead find that drift is largely deterministic and therefore learnable, as holding the noisy state fixed while varying only the history reveals a counterfactual velocity gap that is ~95% systematic across noise seeds. Based on this observation, we introduce Clean Forcing, which trains a 5.9M-parameter LoRA corrector on the frozen base model to regress this gap, with a closed-loop stage that adds DAgger-style aggregation and a drift-contraction objective to expose the corrector to its own rollouts. Because the clean histories can be the base model's own single-shot generations, training needs no real videos, and the merged corrector adds no inference cost. In 50 s text-to-video generation, Clean Forcing reduces {\Delta}-drift from +11.3 to +1.65 without real video and to -0.16 with 40 real clips, improving on the best published result of +3.57 with over 150x less data. Extensive experiments demonstrate that Clean Forcing outperforms prior methods in aesthetic quality and data efficiency with reduced drift for long-horizon video generation.

Title: Formalization of Rényi Differential Privacy in Isabelle/HOL (Draft version)

  • Authors: Tetsuya Sato
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.LO
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25636
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.25636

  • Keywords: privacy
  • Abstract: Differential privacy (DP) is a gold-standard definition of privacy designed to evaluate the privacy guarantee of databases. Rényi differential privacy (RDP) is an important variant of DP that gives tight evaluations of machine learning algorithms. These formulations are easy to understand, but evaluating privacy for actual databases is complicated. Even a small change in the database can break its privacy, and evaluations of DP and RDP themselves are complicated. In particular, RDP evaluations in recent studies have become much harder. Therefore, formal verification of them has been studied for over a decade. In this paper, we propose an Isabelle/HOL library for formalizing Rényi differential privacy in a general setting. We first formalize RDP for continuous distributions. As a technical basis, we formalize several properties of convex functions and $f$-divergences for nonnegative convex functions. We formalize basic properties of RDP, conversion laws from RDP to DP, the moment accountants in DP, and the Gaussian mechanism and its RDP. Finally, we discuss the stochastic gradient descent mechanism with Gaussian noise and formalize a rough evaluation of its RDP and DP.

  • Abstract: Medical image registration is a fundamental task for aligning anatomical structures across images. Most recent learning-based methods directly predict correspondences in a coarse-to-fine manner, but often propagate intermediate estimates across scales without explicitly weighting correspondence evidence, which can allow coarse-scale errors to persist during refinement. To address these challenges, we propose ACorD, an adaptive correspondence discovery framework designed to mitigate error propagation with an attention-guided process. By integrating adaptive offset prediction with selective, attention-based aggregation of correspondence evidence, ACorD enables accurate deformation refinement. Extensive experiments on four public medical registration benchmarks demonstrate that adaptive correspondence discovery within a multi-scale refinement framework improves registration robustness across diverse anatomical structures.

Title: PoSTCache: From Forecasting to Postdictive Smoothing for Revisable Diffusion Caching

  • Authors: Liwei Lan, Hui Chen, Jungong Han, Guiguang Ding
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25664
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.25664
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.25664]] PoSTCache: From Forecasting to Postdictive Smoothing for Revisable Diffusion Caching(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25664)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Diffusion and flow matching models achieve impressive visual generation quality but remain computationally expensive due to iterative sampling. Caching-based acceleration provides a practical training-free solution by reusing or predicting intermediate features. However, existing methods largely enforce a forward-only information flow: approximations produced for skipped model evaluations are immediately committed to the sampling trajectory and cannot benefit from feedback generated by later full-computation steps. We propose \textbf{PoSTCache} (\emph{Postdictive Smoothing Trajectory Cache}), a training-free and plug-and-play framework that reformulates diffusion caching as revisable trajectory inference. PoSTCache first uses an existing caching strategy to construct forward proposals for skipped steps. At a later full-computation step, the discrepancy between the refreshed prediction and its forward proposal yields a correction signal, which PoSTCache propagates backward to refine and reintegrate the preceding predictions. Viewing full-computation steps as sources of corrective feedback also changes how they should be selected. We therefore introduce the Posterior Variance Reduction (PVR) strategy, which estimates cross-timestep dependencies from offline denoising trajectories and selects anchors according to their expected reduction in surrogate trajectory uncertainty rather than local approximation error alone. Experiments on representative image and video generation models show that PoSTCache consistently improves the quality--efficiency trade-off over representative caching methods.

Title: Frechet Forcing: Training Long-Horizon Streaming World Models from Short Clips

  • Authors: Yian Zhao, Yu Yan, Hongcan Guo, Ruochong Zheng, Jian Zhang, Jie Chen
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25666
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.25666
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.25666]] Frechet Forcing: Training Long-Horizon Streaming World Models from Short Clips(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25666)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: Streaming video world models generate the future chunk by chunk, conditioned on their own past and a stream of actions. Trained on short clips, they drift once a rollout exceeds the training horizon, as prediction errors re-enter the context and compound. A common remedy is to train the model on its own rollouts via distribution matching distillation (DMD). For action-conditioned world models, the teacher becomes a bottleneck: an external video model is agnostic to the actions, whereas the model's own pretrained network drifts in the same manner as the student. We observe that a long rollout need not match a teacher, provided it remains a realistic continuation of its own scene that follows the commanded actions. Realism and scene fidelity can be measured against real-data statistics, which remain valid at any horizon, while action following is preserved by the model's conditional generation loss. We propose Fréchet Forcing, which fine-tunes a world model together with its deployed multi-step sampler under three constraints. Generative anchoring applies the flow-matching loss after a self-generated history ending on real data. Context fidelity, a Fréchet distance (FD) on latent statistics of the free rollout centred on each clip's own ground truth, keeps every rollout faithful to its scene. Reality matching, an FD to real frames in a frozen perceptual space, keeps the generated frames realistic. Because all three targets are derived from short real clips without comparing a rollout against a long recorded future, training requires no teacher, no online critic and no long videos. Fine-tuning MiniWorld-1B, pretrained on 253-frame clips, for at most 36 GPU-hours on 381-frame clips yields rollouts that remain stable beyond 4,000 frames and continue to follow their actions in both robot manipulation and camera-controlled exploration.

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Layer-level pruning is attractive for large language model (LLM) deployment as it reduces the model size while preserving a dense architecture that can be efficiently executed on standard hardware. Current methods typically follow a one-shot paradigm, which first removes selected layers to reach a target model size and then performs additional training to recover model capabilities. However, pruning directly to the target size can substantially degrade reasoning capabilities, making subsequent recovery more difficult under a limited training budget. Moreover, this paradigm separates layer selection from recovery, limiting the use of recovery information to guide selection and potentially hindering the recovery of reasoning capabilities. To address these limitations, we propose LOOP, a Layer-level Recovery-Oriented On-Policy Pruning framework that interleaves pruning and recovery across multiple shots. In each shot, LOOP removes a single layer and performs short on-policy distillation, mitigating the abrupt degradation in reasoning capabilities associated with one-shot pruning. To guide layer selection in subsequent shots, LOOP reuses on-policy trajectories and optimization signals from recovery to jointly assess each remaining layer's reasoning contribution and recovery sensitivity, thereby making layer selection recovery-aware. Evaluations on several benchmarks show that LOOP consistently outperforms one-shot baselines in average accuracy across different model scales and pruning ratios.

  • Abstract: Linear stochastic approximation (SA) is widely used to estimate fixed points from noisy observations, but most finite-time analyses assume that the underlying operator remains fixed. Title: Learning to Couple: Context-Adaptive Joint Denoising for Diffusion Language Models

  • Authors: Zilei Shao, Ian Li, Heng Zhao, Kewei Lian, Guy Van den Broeck, Zhe Zeng, Anji Liu
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25723

  • Abstract: Diffusion language models accelerate generation by predicting multiple tokens in parallel, but factorized denoisers treat simultaneous predictions as conditionally independent. Joint modeling addresses the source of the problem, yet existing methods attach separately learned layers to frozen denoisers, leaving the backbone unadapted to the joint objective and the coupling parameters fixed across contexts.   • Abstract: Automated essay scoring (AES) using large language models (LLMs) offers promising use cases for language assessment, yet high-stakes testing requires scores that are both controllable and verifiable, which current LLM-based scorers lack. Given that, we investigate whether LLM-based scorers form distinct internal representations for individual scoring rubrics and whether these representations can be independently controlled. Probing and steering 25 open-weight base LLMs (0.13B-10.31B parameters; 14 families) on human-scored BESTEP English writing tasks, we find that holistic scores are decodable from model activations for the longer tasks (Parts 2 and 3) and only weakly for the shortest (Part 1). Rather than controlling distinct evaluative dimensions, the rubric directions share a dominant lever whose rubric-specific parts are not selective, which may mirror the halo structure of human ratings. Our contributions are two-fold. First, we show that analytic scoring rubrics designed for human raters do not behave as independent controls in base LLMs, even among the seven models whose scores agree best with human raters ($r \geq 0.6$). Second, we establish a methodology that shows that score agreement and decodability do not imply rubric-level control in LLM-based assessment.

Title: Anatomy of Off-Policy Policy Gradient: Importance Sampling, KL Regularization, and Baselines

  • Authors: Haoqun Cao, Yurun Yuan, Tengyang Xie
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25725
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.25725
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.25725]] Anatomy of Off-Policy Policy Gradient: Importance Sampling, KL Regularization, and Baselines(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25725)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Reinforcement learning has become a central tool for large language model (LLM) post-training, where policy gradient methods are routinely deployed off-policy, even though vanilla policy gradient assumes on-policy sampling. We study when such off-policy deployment is justified, and what roles its core ingredients, including importance sampling, KL regularization, and baselines, play in handling the resulting distribution shift. We show that for a general class of off-policy policy gradient updates, consistency, in the sense that the optimal policy remains a stationary point, requires one of two structures at the optimal policy: trajectory-level importance weighting, which suffers from the well-known curse of horizon, or KL regularization with a baseline equal to the optimal soft value, instantiated by the grouped mean. Focusing on the KL route, we identify it with the group-relative policy gradient (GRPG) update, establish an equivalence to a trajectory-level Bellman residual minimization objective, and obtain a finite-sample regret bound under all-policy coverage. This also gives a theoretical account of a variant of the empirically successful GRPO objective. We further uncover a hidden role of baselines in the off-policy setting: a constant shift in the baseline implements pessimism, the standard remedy in offline RL for partial data coverage. We instantiate this via the asymmetric REINFORCE objective, and connect it to the game-theoretic offline RL framework, showing a regret bound with single-policy coverage. Together, these results offer a unified theoretical view of off-policy policy gradient in LLM post-training and connect it to classical ideas in offline RL.

Title: No Matter Who You Are: Addressivity and Sycophancy in Large Language Models

  • Authors: Seungjong Sun, Hyunmin Jun, Minsik Yoon, Eungu Lee, Choongwon Kang, Young Seok Woo, Jang Hyun Kim

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Sycophancy, where an LLM becomes overly agreeable, deferential, or flattering toward the user, has been identified as a major failure mode. While post-training has been implicated as a cause, how it internally gives rise to sycophantic behavior remains insufficiently understood. Post-training transforms the model's output from text completion into utterances directed toward an addressee, thereby giving the output \textit{addressivity}. We hypothesize that the accompanying pressure to follow the addressee's instructions and align with their preferences links this addressivity to sycophancy. Finally, through a controlled post-training experiment, we find that models are sycophantic not toward a specific addressee but toward any recognized addressee, suggesting that the presence of an addressee, rather than the addressee's identity, is what elicits sycophancy.

  • Abstract: LLM unlearning is essential for removing sensitive or unwanted knowledge while preserving useful capabilities. Title: How Much Does One Wrong Answer Cost? Error Propagation in Constraint Pruning with Noisy LLM Oracles

  • Authors: Zhengtao Gui (University of California, Irvine), Weining Shen (University of California, Irvine)
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25739
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.25739
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.25739]] How Much Does One Wrong Answer Cost? Error Propagation in Constraint Pruning with Noisy LLM Oracles(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25739)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Constraint pruning saves queries to a large language model (LLM) or another noisy oracle by skipping each yes/no question implied by answers accepted earlier, for example through transitivity. Entity-resolution systems and LLM-based rankers use this idea. However, their analyses typically assume a correct oracle. We argue that pruning should be judged by its risk when the oracle errs, since an accepted wrong answer spreads to every question it implies. When each answer is wrong independently with small probability $\varepsilon$, the first-order risk is set by how many facts one wrong answer corrupts on average. We consider two kinds of constraints. For implication rules, as in taxonomies, pruning in a random order adds no first-order risk: a wrong answer that would change $k$ facts is accepted with probability $1/k$, so it costs one fact on average. Rules with $s$ premises break this balance: a wrong answer at a premise shared by $r$ rules can cost order $r^{1-1/s}$ facts. For entity resolution with $m$ records of one entity, the first-order risk equals $\varepsilon$ times the Wiener index (the total pairwise distance) of the spanning tree built by the query order. Pivot orders, which first compare one record with all others, amplify errors by less than 2 and are optimal; random orders by order $m^{1/3}$; the common most-similar-first order is the worst in one dimension and, for large $m$, worse than random in two. With several entities, the cost of wrongly merging two does not depend on how many others exist. Simulations agree with every predicted constant within sampling error.

Title: Remember Less, Adapt Longer: Bounded-State In-Context Reinforcement Learning with Recurrent Summary Memory

  • Authors: Jiawen Wang, Cheng Xue, Peng Zhang, Jochen Renz
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25758
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.25758
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.25758]] Remember Less, Adapt Longer: Bounded-State In-Context Reinforcement Learning with Recurrent Summary Memory(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25758)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: In-context reinforcement learning (ICRL) enables agents to adapt to new tasks without parameter updates, relying on memory to retain what they learn from experience. For agents operating continually, existing approaches using memory trade off bounded cost against access to past experience. Approaches using growing memories, such as full-history attention and accumulating summaries, incur storage and per-decision costs that increase with task length. Fixed-size memories bound storage but restrict access: sliding windows discard older steps, while recurrent networks compress even recent interactions into a hidden state. We introduce Recurrent Summary Memory (RSM), a Transformer policy that attends exactly to a short working buffer of recent interactions and carries a fixed-capacity summary of earlier experience. When the buffer fills, RSM rewrites the summary from its previous contents and the buffer, then clears the buffer, so deployment memory and per-decision cost stay $O(1)$ in task length. On long-running partially observable tasks, frozen RSM policies sustain their adapted performance for eight times the training horizon with about 1% of full-history memory, ending ahead of every evaluated baseline while growing memories deteriorate. When the task configuration changes mid-run, RSM re-adapts better than growing and recurrent memories. Clearing the summary or blocking the policy's access to it removes its benefit, while swapping in another task's summary steers behaviour towards that task. Together, these results show that our fixed-capacity summary can sustain in-context adaptation far beyond the training horizon.

Title: Diffusion Language Models Change Speed Across Tasks and Languages

  • Authors: Mohsen Fayyaz, Ali Modarressi, Daniel Israel, Lucas Bandarkar, Nanyun Peng
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25835
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.25835
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.25835]] Diffusion Language Models Change Speed Across Tasks and Languages(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25835)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Diffusion language models can write several tokens in one forward pass, and they decide during generation how many passes to spend. Two outputs of the same token length can therefore vary a lot in cost. To understand the underlying factors of this cost variation, we analyze two diffusion models across 10 tasks. We find that the number of forward passes per token depends heavily on the task. Text copied or lightly edited from the input is cheap, while open-ended writing is costly. In DiffusionGemma, generating a token for story writing requires more than three times as many forward passes as for grammar correction. Two output properties explain much of this gap: how much of it is new relative to the input, and how rare its words are within the task. The same holds within a single response when thinking is involved. The final answer is cheap when it repeats the model's reasoning trace and costly when it adds something new. In multilingual generation, we find that highly tokenized languages also require more passes per token, adding to the cost they already pay with more tokens. Finally, cost tracks correctness: on the same math problem, wrong answers take more passes per token than correct ones, and weighting candidate answers by decoding speed beats majority voting by up to 4.2 points at no extra cost. Taken together, our results show that diffusion's computational advantage depends on how much of the output is already determined, while its decoding effort offers a practical signal for making inference more efficient and reliable.

Title: SWIFT: Self-guided Weak-to-strong dIFfusion models via Target extrapolation

  • Authors: Yanjin He, Cuifeng Shen, Wanzhou Lei, Maohua Li, Tao Lan, Kan Liu, Hanlin Tang, Qing Qu
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25844
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.25844
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.25844]] SWIFT: Self-guided Weak-to-strong dIFfusion models via Target extrapolation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25844)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Training high-quality diffusion models is computationally expensive, and inference-time guidance often adds further sampling cost. In this work, we introduce Swift, a simple yet effective self-guided training method that uses a frozen weak denoiser to improve a stronger model without adding inference overhead. For each noisy input, Swift extrapolates the clean regression target away from the weak denoiser's prediction, amplifying components that remain underfit. The weak guide can be an early training checkpoint, requires no backpropagation during main-model training, and is discarded afterward. The resulting model retains the original sampler and supports both conditional and unconditional generation without label dropout or an unconditional branch. We explain this mechanism through a spectral analysis of finite-time population gradient flow in an RKHS. With an early-stopped weak guide, we prove that a nonempty range of extrapolation strengths yields strictly lower posterior-mean error than matched standard training, despite shifting the regression target away from the posterior mean. Experiments on CIFAR-10 and ImageNet-256 demonstrate improvements across model scales and representations. In one ImageNet-256 configuration, Swift surpasses the baseline's best FID using roughly half the total training compute, including guide preparation and training overhead. On RAEv2, it achieves FID 1.15 without inference-time guidance and 0.99 with guidance. These results establish target extrapolation as an effective approach to weak-to-strong diffusion training with efficient single-model inference.

Title: Measurement Validity and Representation Dynamics of Confidence Signals in Large Language Models

  • Authors: Debojeet Ghosh, Shreyansh Kumar, Ayushman Garg
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25846
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.25846
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.25846]] Measurement Validity and Representation Dynamics of Confidence Signals in Large Language Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25846)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Whether a language model "knows what it knows" matters for trustworthy deployment and for deciding when it should abstain. Prior work usually measures this through one channel: verbalized confidence, a linear probe on hidden states, or agreement among sampled answers (semantic entropy), and often treats these as interchangeable. We compare all three in a pre-registered audit of five Qwen2.5 models (0.5B-7B, including a base/instruct pair at 7B) on factual retrieval (PopQA, SimpleQA) and multi-step reasoning (GSM8K, MATH). Second, verbalized confidence is unstable: slightly different prompt templates yield scores that barely correlate. Third, Expected Calibration Error is misleading: verbal confidence looks well calibrated because it clusters near the base rate while discriminating poorly, whereas sampling agreement has worse ECE but about twice the resolution. Of five pre-registered hypotheses, two were falsified, one was partly supported, one was not supported, and one could not be tested because the base model's verbal confidence was degenerate.

Title: Residual-Guided Adaptive Tucker Rank Allocation for Parameter-Efficient MCI Conversion Prediction

  • Authors: Guanghua He, Hancan Zhu, Gaohang Yu, An Zhang
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25873
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.25873
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.25873]] Residual-Guided Adaptive Tucker Rank Allocation for Parameter-Efficient MCI Conversion Prediction(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25873)
  • Keywords: segmentation
  • Abstract: Allocating limited trainable capacity across convolutional layers is a central challenge in parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) of pretrained three-dimensional (3D) networks. We propose Residual-Guided Adaptive Tucker Rank Allocation, which jointly selects input- and output-channel ranks under a fixed adapter-parameter budget while preserving the spatial kernel modes. To guide rank selection, we learn task-specific proxy updates from downstream training data and approximate them using candidate Tucker rank pairs. A perturbation analysis bounds the output discrepancy between the fixed proxy network and its Tucker approximation in terms of layerwise reconstruction residuals, motivating residual-based allocation. We normalize each squared residual by the corresponding proxy-update energy to compare relative approximation errors across layers. Dynamic programming minimizes their sum under a global budget constraint that accounts for both channel factors and the Tucker core. This yields a globally optimal allocation over the specified candidate rank pairs. We evaluate the method on the Alzheimer's Disease Neuroimaging Initiative (ADNI) cohort for predicting conversion from mild cognitive impairment to Alzheimer's disease within 36 months, using hippocampal segmentation pretraining. Across five random seeds, the proposed method achieves a mean area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUC) of 0.7706, compared with 0.7239 for fixed-ratio Tucker adaptation and 0.7430 for full fine-tuning. The total number of trainable parameters is limited to approximately 0.618 million, accounting for only 1.45% of the full model. Adaptive Tucker rank allocation thus provides an effective approach to parameter-efficient transfer learning in 3D medical imaging.

Title: Budgeted Attack Scheduling for LLM Red-Teaming: The Roles of Static Priors, Semantic Guidance, and Online Feedback

  • Authors: Zhicheng Fang, Han Sun, Jiachen Lei, Xinyi Fang, Zongmin Zhang, Chenxu Fu, Sizhe Fang, Wei Xu
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25897
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.25897
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.25897]] Budgeted Attack Scheduling for LLM Red-Teaming: The Roles of Static Priors, Semantic Guidance, and Online Feedback(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25897)
  • Keywords: defense, attack, robust, large language model
  • Abstract: As jailbreak attacks against large language models (LLMs) become increasingly diverse, red-teaming has come to rely on large libraries of jailbreak prompts and attack strategies to evaluate the robustness of victim models and their defense settings. However, attack effectiveness is highly condition-dependent: an attack that succeeds under one victim-defense setting may fail under another, while exhaustively evaluating the full attack library for each setting incurs substantial token and monetary costs. This motivates studying how jailbreak attacks should be prioritized under a limited attempt budget for efficient robustness evaluation. In this work, we formalize this problem as budgeted attack scheduling over a fixed attack library and investigate three complementary signals under a fixed victim-defense condition: (1) static historical attack success rate (ASR) priors; (2) semantic guidance from LLMs; and (3) online feedback from earlier queries. Building on these signals, we design four scheduling strategies and evaluate them across three victim models and three defense settings on GPT-5.2. Extensive experiments clearly characterize the conditions under which each signal is most effective. Historical ASR provides a strong initialization, but its reliability degrades under defense-induced distribution shifts. Semantic guidance consistently improves or matches static prior-based scheduling in the cold-start regime, while online feedback becomes particularly valuable when transferred priors become stale. Semantic guidance and online feedback can also partially compensate for missing historical priors. Taken together, these findings characterize budgeted red-teaming as an attack scheduling problem in which historical priors, semantic guidance, and online feedback play complementary roles.

  • Abstract: Sequential knowledge editing in Large Language Models (LLMs) often leads to a degradation of general capabilities due to cumulative parameter interference.   • Abstract: As coding models and agent harnesses become more capable, reliable security verification is becoming a central bottleneck. Title: Keep the Lineages Alive: Diversity-Preserving SMC for Diffusion LLM Decoding

  • Authors: Minoo Ahmadi, Seyedarmin Azizi, Mehdi Kamal, Massoud Pedram
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL, stat.AP, stat.ML
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25932
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.25932
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.25932]] Keep the Lineages Alive: Diversity-Preserving SMC for Diffusion LLM Decoding(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25932)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Inference-time sequential Monte Carlo (SMC) decoding can improve the reasoning accuracy of masked diffusion language models (diffusion LLMs) without post-training by resampling a population of partial generations according to a reward. Its effectiveness therefore depends on the resampling step, which must concentrate computation on promising candidates while retaining enough diversity to explore alternative reasoning paths. The reward can be internal, the decoder's own confidence on the committed prefix, or external, a process reward model scoring that prefix. Under either reward, multinomial resampling can disrupt this balance by duplicating high-weight particles and discarding lineages that may still reach a correct solution. The effective sample size (ESS) does not register this loss: multinomial resampling resets it to its maximum even as distinct lineages disappear. To retain such lineages, we introduce lineage-preserving SMC (LP-SMC), which combines Chopthin resampling with an input weight floor. Instead of equalizing weights, Chopthin bounds the ratio between the largest and smallest weights it returns and carries the unequal weights forward. The floor limits how far below the maximum a weight may fall before resampling so that lineages with low early weight keep a chance to recover. Across mathematical-reasoning and code-generation benchmarks on two base models, LP-SMC raises oracle coverage, the fraction of problems with at least one correct particle, in every setting, by 1.8 to 25.6 points, and raises end-to-end accuracy under a common reward-model selector in eleven of twelve settings, by up to 7.9 points on LLaDA-1.5 and 18.3 on Dream-7B. The coverage gain persists under the external reward model, which suggests that diversity-preserving resampling and the guidance signal contribute separately to inference-time search.

On the fixed baseline we run nine inference-time corrections of our own (none beat it) and dissect why the adversarial split resists logit-level intervention. The quality of the solutions depends critically on the interplay and consensus between the inverse problem solver and the generative model sampler. We revisit a recently introduced flow-based PnP framework, which we refer to as PnP-CF (Consensus Flow), and extend its application to nonlinear inverse problems, including those with learned neural-network forward operators. PnP-CF combines a flow-matching denoiser with (i) ADMM-like dual variable updates and (ii) a time-adaptive pullback onto the intermediate flow probability path that combines a stochastic and a deterministic component. We discuss how these two features stabilize the algorithm and promote consensus, and demonstrate on a set of experiments that PnP-CF not only solves standard linear inverse problems with quality at least comparable to state-of-the-art baselines, but also extends gracefully to nonlinear inverse problems, including forward operators realized by pretrained neural networks, for which existing methods produce unsatisfactory results or fail completely.

Title: SPLASH: Contrastive Audit Pressure for Detecting and Localizing Security Hallucinations in Generated Code

  • Authors: Mirza Masfiqur Rahman, MD Ajwad Akil, Imtiaz Karim, Ashish Kundu, Ramana Kompella, Elisa Bertino
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.LG, cs.SE
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26001
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.26001
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.26001]] SPLASH: Contrastive Audit Pressure for Detecting and Localizing Security Hallucinations in Generated Code(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26001)
  • Keywords: secure, security, large language model
  • Abstract: Code generated by large language models often passes every functional test while remaining insecure. These security hallucinations are common, written with high confidence, and repeated across samples, so neither the generator's own uncertainty nor static analyzers reliably catch them. We introduce SPLASH, which is guided by a new measure called audit pressure. It contrasts teacher-forced log-probabilities for the generated program under a security review and a style review. Aggregated to statements and programs, and grounded in a probabilistic model of competing edits, audit pressure allows SPLASH to choose the secure program among a model's candidate programs and locate the vulnerable statement, with no model training, CWE-specific rules, or internal access to the generating model. On 63,684 programs generated by 12 models for 899 tasks, SPLASH picks secure code in 74% of cases, places the vulnerable statement first in 58% of insecure programs, and chooses better than a frontier LLM judge.

Title: SpanGuard: Decoupling Instruction Localization from Authorization for Lightweight IPI Defense

  • Authors: Yinxiang Wang, Zehua Ma, Kejiang Chen, Weiming Zhang, Han Fang
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26013
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.26013
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.26013]] SpanGuard: Decoupling Instruction Localization from Authorization for Lightweight IPI Defense(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26013)
  • Keywords: defense, attack, segmentation
  • Abstract: LLM-based guardrails are effective against indirect prompt injection (IPI), but their performance depends heavily on model capability: as the guardrail model becomes smaller or weaker, detection degrades substantially. This raises a natural question: can we retain strong IPI defense while relying only on a small LLM? To understand why, we revisit what an LLM guardrail actually does. Detecting IPI requires simultaneously locating potentially injected instructions within heterogeneous tool outputs and judging whether they are authorized by the user's request. We find that the difficulty for small models arises largely from satisfying both requirements jointly, rather than from either subproblem alone. This suggests a different path to lightweight defense: explicitly decoupling localization from decision-making. In particular, localization needs no strong language-model reasoning, since injected instructions are often semantically discontinuous with their surrounding content and can be partitioned into local regions via lightweight unsupervised segmentation. Once the observation is partitioned this way, the remaining authorization check becomes a localized judgment that a small LLM can handle. Building on this insight, we design a lightweight IPI guardrail that performs attack-agnostic localization followed by block-level authorization judgment with a small language model. By separating where to look from what to decide, our approach substantially reduces the capability required of the guardrail model while preserving strong defense performance. Empirically, our guardrail achieves a 0.32% attack success rate with negligible loss in benign utility while remaining effective with substantially smaller models. These results show that strong IPI defense need not rely on strong LLMs when localization and decision-making are explicitly decoupled.

Title: FrameGRPO: Incentivizing Video Reasoning via Multi-Frame Rollouts and Temporal Credit

  • Authors: Jiaxuan Sun, Biao Wang, Shuai Li
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26022
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.26022
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.26022]] FrameGRPO: Incentivizing Video Reasoning via Multi-Frame Rollouts and Temporal Credit(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26022)
  • Keywords: robust, large language model
  • Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards, particularly Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), has improved video reasoning in multimodal large language models. However, standard video GRPO varies only linguistic trajectories while conditioning every rollout in a group on the same sampled frames, which can produce homogeneous rewards and weaken group-relative learning when the shared visual evidence is insufficient or distracting. We propose \textbf{FrameGRPO}, a simple and effective reinforcement-learning framework that requires neither auxiliary networks nor changes to the model architecture. Specifically, FrameGRPO combines two lightweight components: (1) \textbf{Multi-Frame Rollouts}, which use only uniform temporal subsampling of a single maximal-frame decode to construct aligned views that diversify visual evidence and reasoning trajectories; and (2) \textbf{Gated Temporal Efficiency Credit}, which selectively reinforces a correct lower-ratio trajectory when all rollouts in a fixed full-ratio reference subset fail, without imposing an unconditional preference for fewer frames. Extensive experiments across diverse video understanding and reasoning benchmarks demonstrate robust overall improvements over standard GRPO, highlighting temporal-view diversification as an effective mechanism for exploration and credit assignment in video MLLMs.

Ablations support trajectory supervision, and routing controls favor LTE over random decisions at nearly matched latency.   • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Looped transformers reuse a shared block of layers to increase computational depth without increasing the number of trainable parameters. To study this tradeoff systematically, we jointly vary model size, training tokens, and forward and backward loop counts across nearly 600 training runs and fit a unified scaling law that captures the effects of looping and truncation through effective parameters. Title: Provable Adversarial Detection: Prime Quantization meets Gromov--Wasserstein

  • Authors: Manohar Kaul, Sadbhavana Babar, Aditya Ramesh, Aditya Saibewar, Fumiyoshi Kasahara, Maki Inui, Hiroaki Kurihara
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26102
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.26102
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.26102]] Provable Adversarial Detection: Prime Quantization meets Gromov--Wasserstein(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26102)
  • Keywords: attack
  • Abstract: Adversarial vulnerability persists across modern vision architectures from CNNs to vision language models (VLMs), yet existing detection methods rely on heuristics without theoretical guarantees. We address the fundamental question of when adversarial perturbations can be provably detected from a geometric perspective. Our key insight is that adversarial perturbations cannot simultaneously preserve geometric structure across spaces with fundamentally different properties. Accordingly, we construct two such complementary metric spaces. First, we use a standard CNN embedding space $Z$, where adversarial samples exhibit significant displacement patterns. Second, we build a novel prime-quantized space $P$, that absorbs small perturbations through number-theoretic discretization, resulting in minimal displacement, while preserving discriminability. We then leverage the geometric discrepancies across spaces $Z$ and $P$ to detect adversarial samples. To the best of our knowledge, we establish the first rigorous separation theory for adversarial detection, proving that adversarial samples create unavoidable geometric inconsistencies across both spaces. Our framework provides theoretical guarantees including pixel-level absorption bounds, neighborhood diameter concentration, Gromov-Wasserstein (GW) separation theorems, and practical risk control. Extensive experiments validate our theoretical predictions and achieve consistently strong detection performance across a wide range of attack types and model families.

Title: Optimal Multi-Kernel Combination for Online Multi-Label Classification

  • Authors: Tingting Zhai, Wei Liu, Renjian Yu
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26123
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.26123
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.26123]] Optimal Multi-Kernel Combination for Online Multi-Label Classification(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26123)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Multi-label classification is particularly challenging in streaming scenarios, where data arrives sequentially. Online kernel methods offer a promising solution. However, prevailing methods rely on a single preselected kernel, making them prone to suboptimal choices since the global data distribution is unknown in advance in online settings. Although the recent method, Online Kernel Selection for Online Multi-label Classification (OKS-OMC), enables data-driven kernel selection, it fails to fully harness the complementary information from multiple kernels and encounters the curse of kernelization. To address these limitations, this paper introduces a novel online multi-kernel combination method for large-scale multi-label classification. First, an efficient and robust online single-kernel multi-label method is designed. Second, learning an optimal convex combination of multiple multi-label kernel classifiers is formalized as a structural risk minimization problem. An improved alternating optimization procedure is developed, in which the combination coefficients are updated by directly minimizing the objective function, while each kernel classifier is updated by minimizing a surrogate upper bound. This procedure decouples the learning of kernel classifiers from combination coefficients, allowing independent classifier updates via our single-kernel method and accelerating convergence to high-quality solutions. Our multi-kernel method is proven to asymptotically converge to the optimal fixed convex combination of the best fixed single-kernel models chosen in hindsight. Ablation studies confirm that our method's components outperform their counterparts in OKS-OMC. Comprehensive evaluations across 11 datasets and seven metrics demonstrate our method's overall superiority over existing online multi-label methods.

Title: TriTD: Tri-Partite Trajectory-Distribution Distillation for Real-Time Autoregressive Video Generation

  • Authors: Jia Li, Xurui Peng, Haowei Zhu, Tianyu Zhao, Jiexi Wang, Youwei Zheng, Yuxi Ren, Xinglong Wu, Xing Wang, Hayden Kwok-Hay So
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26165
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.26165
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.26165]] TriTD: Tri-Partite Trajectory-Distribution Distillation for Real-Time Autoregressive Video Generation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26165)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Autoregressive (AR) video diffusion has emerged as a leading paradigm for efficient video generation, yet its practical deployment remains bottlenecked by the trade-off between generation quality and inference efficiency. We attribute this bottleneck to three error sources in AR rollouts: the base model error, the train-inference distribution mismatch, and the upstream error propagation. Building on this decomposition, we derive closed-form upper bounds on the per-step and cumulative errors, providing a principled basis for jointly optimizing generation quality and inference efficiency. Guided by this analysis, we propose TriTD, a Tri-Partite Trajectory-Distribution co-optimized distillation framework with three complementary modules. First, for ODE initialization, we adopt a Next-Node Trajectory Distillation (NTD) objective, which shortens the supervision horizon and admits a lower regression-error floor. Second, we introduce Chunk-Graded Noising (CGN), a monotonically non-decreasing per-chunk noising schedule that helps narrow the expected train-inference noise mismatch and removes redundant KV-cache updates, yielding substantial speedups at no quality cost. Third, we propose Orthogonal-Parallel DMD (OP-DMD), a distribution-matching loss that retains the original DMD supervision while introducing an additional regression constraint in the orthogonal subspace to suppress off-axis drift, improving both training stability and final generation quality. Extensive experiments show that TriTD consistently surpasses existing baselines in visual quality while reaching a real-time throughput of 26.8 FPS on a single H100 GPU, delivering a balanced quality-efficiency solution for interactive streaming video generation.

Title: PhantomTac: Hijacking Robotic Tactile Systems with Deploy-and-forget Tags

  • Authors: Binbin Zhang, Yifeng Zhang, Xiaomeng Chen, Dongyao Chen
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.RO
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26174
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.26174
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.26174]] PhantomTac: Hijacking Robotic Tactile Systems with Deploy-and-forget Tags(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26174)
  • Keywords: security, defense, attack, robust, steal
  • Abstract: Tactile perception is crucial for precise robotic manipulation, providing an important foundation for dexterous, closed-loop force control. Among various tactile sensing techniques, magnetic tactile sensors stand out thanks to their fine-grained, three-dimensional force perception capability and strong robustness against visual occlusion. In this study, we discover a physical loophole called PhantomTac in magnetic tactile sensors. The attacker can alter the magnetic field, thus spoofing the force interpreted by magnetic tactile sensors. The attacker can stealthily deploy such an attack by simply attaching a thin (the thickness is around 0.5 mm), battery-free magnetic tag to the surface of an object. Hence, the attack tag can be easily concealed by a barcode sticker. Such an attack scheme is "deploy-and-forget" -- the attacker can deploy the tag and leave. When a robotic hand is picking up an object, it may severely damage the object. Specifically, spoofing the normal force can crush the object, whereas spoofing the shear force can lead to a loose grip, thus dropping the object. Compared with directly sabotaging the robotic hand, PhantomTac is much more concealed as the attacker does not need access to the device. We performed extensive reverse-engineering efforts to characterize the sensing mechanism of commercial-off-the-shelf tactile sensors and proposed a practical tag design. End-to-end evaluations conducted on real robots and robotic arms demonstrate that our attack can bypass existing anti-interference mechanisms and effectively hijack their closed-loop control processes, achieving an attack success rate exceeding 95% when the grasping-position offset is bounded within 8 mm. Finally, based on the limitations of existing anti-magnetic interference mechanisms, we propose a multimodal-consistency defense mechanism to mitigate such physical-layer security threats.

However, predictions produced with sparse attention become sources for temporal reuse, coupling their errors along the sampling trajectory. We observe that improving the accuracy of these sources recovers fidelity without changing the reuse schedule, and that sparsification errors remain directionally aligned across nearby steps, suggesting that correction information can be shared rather than recomputed. These observations motivate RECAP, a training-free method that couples sparse attention with hierarchical caching through reciprocal correction, adaptively coordinated by online error feedback. Cached dense refinement corrects omitted interactions in current sparse estimates, while fresh sparse statistics track input changes and regulate the aging correction. The resulting predictions then renew the sources for whole-step prediction reuse, allowing prediction history to advance between dense refreshes. We evaluate RECAP on multiple video diffusion models against sparse-attention methods, caching methods, and their direct compositions. On HunyuanVideo at 720p 5-second generation, RECAP achieves a 3.05x speedup and outperforms all tested direct compositions in both fidelity and inference speed.

  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Video-Language Models (VLMs) excel at visual recognition but struggle with causal reasoning. Title: Learning SOCP Solver Dynamics with Graph Neural Networks

  • Authors: Adam Ghalem, Lou Salaün, Davide Rinaldi, Fragkiskos D. Malliaros
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26285
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.26285
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.26285]] Learning SOCP Solver Dynamics with Graph Neural Networks(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26285)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Second-order cone programs (SOCPs) are a fundamental class of convex optimization problems widely applied across engineering domains. Classical interior-point methods are robust and accurate, but their iterative nature can be costly in real-time or repeated-solve settings. This has motivated learning-based solvers that approximate optimizer solutions with neural networks. Recent work has increasingly studied graph neural networks (GNNs) for linear, quadratic, and second-order cone optimization, leveraging their ability to model sparse relational structure among variables, constraints, and objective functions. Most existing GNN-based solvers compress the optimization process into a single forward pass, predicting the optimizer directly without using the intermediate trajectory produced by classical solvers. We introduce SOCPNet, a recursive GNN framework that uses solver trajectories to learn an iterative SOCP solution procedure. A shared GNN model is applied repeatedly, refining the current iterate at each step and enabling inference for a variable number of iterations. Rather than predicting the solution in a single pass, SOCPNet learns from intermediate iterates to progressively refine its estimates using a trajectory-regularized training procedure. We evaluate SOCPNet on multiple datasets spanning standard SOCP problem classes and show that it consistently reduces the final solution error relative to a SOCP-GNN baseline. Finally, we demonstrate how trained SOCPNet models can be used in a hybrid optimization pipeline by warm-starting a classical interior-point solver, reducing solver iterations without loss of accuracy.

Title: Toward Online Robust Zero-Sum Markov Games with Function Approximation

  • Authors: Chenhao Zhou, Jiyu Wei
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26286
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.26286

  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Online robust zero-sum Markov games pose a coupled operator-learning and game-solving problem: from nominal interaction data, the learner must recover a worst-case minimax Bellman operator while maintaining strategic coverage and controlling stage-game approximation error. Existing robust reinforcement-learning theory does not yet provide such an operator-learning perspective beyond specialized multi-agent settings. We develop an optimistic dual robust Bellman framework that combines dual robust Bellman fitting, optimistic minimax planning, backward-validated confidence sets, and exploiter-mix data collection. Our analysis is organized around the robust Bellman residuals induced by the candidate value class and yields a regret reduction that separates exploration complexity, robust operator-estimation error, stage-game solution error, and misspecification. Under explicit interpretable assumptions, we obtain conditional theorem interfaces for general and projected function approximation, together with closed results in several structured regimes, including tabular, epochwise cross-fit linear, self-normalized pure-online linear / finite-rank kernel, and projected RKHS truncation. Small-scale numerical experiments illustrate robustness calibration, strategic coverage, and the return-gap trade-off predicted by the framework.

  • Abstract: Work on efficient large-language-model inference has concentrated almost entirely on reducing the cost of decoding once a request is complete. While a request is being composed it is already partially specified, yet conventional deployments begin no computation until submission.
